**Growth drivers.** The primary-sourced measure of forward demand is in the 10-Q: "We have significantly increased our **supply and capacity commitments from $119 billion last quarter to $279 billion as of July 26, 2026**." **A much larger "backlog" figure circulates in secondary coverage; it is a third-party report and is deliberately not used here — the $279 billion commitment is the filed number.** NVIDIA guided Q3 FY2027 revenue to about $108.0B ±2% and stated it "is not assuming any Data Center compute revenue from China in its outlook" (company press release). AI Clouds, Industrial & Enterprise growing faster than Hyperscale (+138% against +102%) suggests a customer base broadening beyond the largest clouds into neoclouds, sovereign programs and enterprise.

**Company-specific bear case.** **Customer concentration, in NVIDIA's own words from the 10-Q:** "For the second quarter of fiscal year 2027, **one direct customer represented 16% of total revenue**… For the first half of fiscal year 2027, **three direct customers represented 16%, 15%, and 13%** of total revenue", with five direct customers accounting for 22%, 14%, 13%, 11% and 10% of accounts receivable. **NVIDIA names none of these customers, and neither does this document** — but four of the basket's other members are among the largest buyers of AI compute in the world. The filing also discloses indirect exposure: "We estimate that **one AI research and deployment company contributed a meaningful amount of our revenue by purchasing cloud services from our customers**." China revenue including Hong Kong was $7,880M in the quarter against $3,985M a year earlier, but **down for the half — $12,430M against $13,644M** — and the guidance assumes zero China data-center compute. **The export-control position behind those figures is set out in the FY2026 10-K** (year ended 2026-01-25), which puts China including Hong Kong at **$19,677M, or 9.1% of FY2026 revenue, against 19.2% the year before**, and states: "Under the current rules and geopolitical landscape, **we are unable to create and deliver a competitive product for China's data center market** that receives approval from both the USG and the Chinese government. As of the end of fiscal year 2026, we were **effectively foreclosed from competing in China's data center computing/compute market**, and our effective foreclosure from the China market **helped our competitors build larger developer and customer ecosystems to challenge us worldwide**." The same filing records the **$4.5 billion charge** taken in Q1 FY2026 on excess H20 inventory and purchase obligations after the USG imposed a license requirement in April 2025; that a February 2026 license permits "small amounts of H200 products" to specific China-based customers but "**To date, we have not generated any revenue under the H200 licensing program**," with any units subject to a **25% tariff** on importation into the United States; an expectation that the US government "will receive **15% or more** of the revenue generated from licensed sales," with no regulation yet codifying it; revenue from customers headquartered outside the US falling to **31%** of total in FY2026 from 41% and 48% in the two prior years; FY2026 gross margin falling to **71.1%** from 75.0% the year before, partly on the H20 charge; and supply-chain concentration in "**China, Hong Kong, Israel, Korea and Taiwan**." NVIDIA has also made very large equity investments in AI labs that are themselves buyers of its chips; **the dollar values reported for those commitments are third-party reports rather than filing disclosures and are not stated here**, though the 10-Q does disclose **$4.7B of maximum loss exposure to variable interest entities** as of 2026-07-26. The segment reclassification also removes any separate view of the consumer business. **Finally, the weighting itself:** NVIDIA is about 22% of a capitalization-weighted Magnificent Seven and the largest single weight in the S&P 500, but MAG7 holds it at a 14.29% target — **a deliberate underweight of roughly 35% against cap weight** (NVIDIA was ~21.9% of a cap-weighted Magnificent Seven on Reserve's `dtf/exposure` data and ~7.53% of the S&P 500 on 2026-09-16 per slickcharts; illustrative and approximate, and index weights move every day), the mirror image of what equal weighting does to Tesla.

### 6.2 Apple — `AAPL` · Consumer hardware and services · the frontier rented in · live weight ~14.9%

**What it does, by revenue segment.** FY2025 (ended **2025-09-27**, from Apple's 8-K exhibit on SEC EDGAR) total net sales were **$416.161B**: **iPhone $209.586B (~50%)**, **Services $109.158B (~26%)**, **Wearables, Home & Accessories $35.686B (~9%)**, **Mac $33.708B (~8%)**, **iPad $28.023B (~7%)**. Greater China was **$64.377B**, about 15.5% of the year. FY2025 net income was **$112.010B**, diluted EPS **$7.46**. In the latest reported quarter, **FQ3 2026 (ended 2026-06-27, released 2026-07-30, 8-K exhibit)**, net sales were **$109.4B (+16%)** with **iPhone $54.3B (+22%, a June-quarter record)** and **Services $30.7B (also a record)**; gross margin **50.1%**, operating income **$35.7B**, net income **$29.8B**, diluted EPS **$2.02**. Greater China grew to $18.8B from $15.4B.

**Size, as of 2026-09-16.** Market capitalization **~$4.86 trillion** (Reserve `dtf/exposure`), share price **$332.69**, TTM revenue **$466.82B**, P/E **~38** (stockanalysis.com) — the highest multiple of the seven apart from Tesla.

**Role in AI.** Apple is **the AI laggard of the seven, and it has outsourced the frontier to a basket-mate**. On **2026-01-12** Apple and Google announced a multi-year collaboration under which the next generation of Apple Foundation Models will be built on **Google's Gemini models and cloud technology**, powering future Apple Intelligence features including a rebuilt Siri. Apple's own statement: "After careful evaluation, we determined that Google's technology provides the most capable foundation for Apple Foundation Models" (quoted verbatim in MacRumors, 2026-01-12, corroborated by CNBC the same day). **Figures circulating for the price of that deal come only from low-quality secondary sites and are not stated here.** Apple's models still run on-device and on its own Private Cloud Compute.

**Growth drivers.** A strong iPhone cycle (+22% in the June quarter); Services at ~$30.7B a quarter, higher-margin than hardware and now larger than Mac, iPad and Wearables combined; the next generation of Apple Intelligence and a rebuilt, conversational Siri unveiled at WWDC in June 2026. At its **2026-09-09** event Apple announced the iPhone 18 Pro at $1,199 and iPhone 18 Pro Max at $1,299 — $100 above the prior generation — plus a foldable iPhone, with **no base iPhone 18 this cycle** and the cheaper models moved to spring 2027 (reported by MacRumors, AppleInsider and CNBC's live coverage; **the split cadence materially affects quarter-on-quarter comparability**).

**Company-specific bear case.** **Apple completed a CEO transition on 2026-09-01: John Ternus succeeded Tim Cook, Cook became Executive Chairman, and Arthur Levinson moved to lead independent director** (announced 2026-04-20; Apple Newsroom). **This is the largest governance change in the basket**, and the 2026-09-09 event was Ternus's first as chief executive. Apple's frontier AI now depends on another constituent of the same basket — a concentration of counterparty risk *inside* MAG7. Google's payments for the Safari search default remain high-margin revenue whose scale is only estimated in litigation coverage, not disclosed; Judge Mehta's remedies ruling allowed the payments but barred exclusivity and capped any default commitment at 12 months, and both the remedies and the liability rulings are under appeal (reported; outcome open). Greater China at ~15.5% of FY2025 revenue carries tariff, competitive and policy exposure. FQ3 2026 gross margin included roughly **two points of benefit from tariff refunds**, which flatters that comparison. And about half of revenue still comes from one product line.

### 6.3 Alphabet — `GOOGL` · Models, silicon, cloud and distribution · live weight ~14.5%

**What it does, by revenue segment.** Q2 2026 (quarter ended 2026-06-30, released 2026-07-22, from Alphabet's 8-K exhibit): total revenues **$119,796M (+24%)** — **Google Search & other $63,271M (52.8%)**, **Google Cloud $24,768M (+82%, 20.7%)**, **subscriptions, platforms & devices $12,911M (10.8%)**, **YouTube ads $11,055M (9.2%)**, **Google Network $7,303M (6.1%)**, Other Bets $382M. Operating income **$40,770M**, a ~34% margin. Net income **$112,193M** and diluted EPS **$9.11**. Capital expenditures in the quarter were **$(44,924)M** and **free cash flow was negative $5,855M**.

**Size, as of 2026-09-16.** Market capitalization **~$4.23 trillion** on Reserve's `dtf/exposure` read, against ~$4.19 trillion on companiesmarketcap the same day. **The ~0.9% gap is a question of basis, and neither source publishes its own:** Alphabet has two listed classes, **GOOGL** (Class A) and **GOOG** (Class C), and market-cap sources differ on whether the second is included. Neither Reserve nor companiesmarketcap states which basis its figure uses, so the gap should be read as a probable difference of basis rather than a disagreement about price. **MAG7 holds the `GOOGLc` token, which references Class A only** — a small, checkable divergence from the theme's own reference benchmark, which splits Alphabet's equal weight across both classes by free float (see Section 7, *Methodology: equal weight, seven names, two months*). Share price **$346.00** (GOOGL), TTM revenue **$445.87B**, P/E **~17** (stockanalysis.com).

**Role in AI.** Alphabet is **the most vertically integrated AI company in the basket**: it owns the models (Gemini), the silicon (TPU), the cloud, the distribution (Search, Chrome, Android, YouTube, Workspace) and, in DeepMind, the research organization.

**Growth drivers.** Gemini 3, launched 2025-11-18, is widely credited as the inflection for the franchise, and Alphabet crossed a $4 trillion market capitalization in January 2026 (CNBC, 2026-01-12). Cloud grew **82%** to $24.8B in one quarter with cloud operating income of **$8.8B against $2.8B** a year earlier. Seventh-generation **Ironwood** TPUs are reported to have become generally available on Google Cloud in May 2026, and Google is reported to have begun delivering TPU systems into customers' own data centers (investing.com's analysis and other secondary summaries of Alphabet's Q2 2026 call; neither claim is a filing disclosure) — **and all external-TPU volume and customer figures in circulation are likewise third-party reports, none of which is stated here.** The Apple Foundation Models deal (Section 6.2) puts Gemini underneath a very large installed base of Apple devices.

**Company-specific bear case.** **The ~17 P/E is an artifact, not cheapness.** Q2 2026 net income of $112.2B was dominated by roughly $98B of other income, overwhelmingly gains on equity securities; Fortune (2026-07-22) and other outlets attributed those gains chiefly to **Anthropic and SpaceX revaluations**. The net income and EPS are verified from the 8-K; **the attribution is third-party reporting — Alphabet's FY2025 10-K never names Anthropic at all.** These are mark-to-market gains and they can reverse. On spending, Alphabet's FY2025 10-K states: "During the years ended December 31, 2024 and 2025, we spent **$52.5 billion and $91.4 billion on capital expenditures**… In 2026, we expect to significantly increase, relative to 2025, our investment in our technical infrastructure." **The larger 2026 guidance figures circulating in secondary summaries are not stated here**; the filing language and the negative Q2 free cash flow are the verifiable part. Judge Mehta found in 2024 that Google unlawfully maintained a search monopoly; the remedies ruling declined to force a Chrome divestiture but barred exclusivity, and appeals from both sides are live (reported; outcome open). The core ~53% of revenue is the business most exposed to chat-based and agentic answering — including Alphabet's own.

### 6.4 Microsoft — `MSFT` · Enterprise software and Azure · live weight ~14.1%

**What it does, by revenue segment.** FY2026 (twelve months ended **2026-06-30**, released 2026-07-29, from Microsoft Investor Relations): total revenue **$331.8B (+18%)** — **Productivity & Business Processes $140.0B (~42%)** (Microsoft 365, Office, LinkedIn, Dynamics), **Intelligent Cloud $137.8B (~42%)** (Azure, server products, enterprise services), **More Personal Computing $54.1B (~16%)** (Windows, devices, gaming, search advertising). Operating income **$155.2B (+21%)**, net income **$133.7B**, diluted EPS **$17.95**. In Q4 FY2026, revenue was **$90.0B (+18%)** with Intelligent Cloud **+32%**, More Personal Computing **−4%**, Microsoft Cloud **$59.3B (+27%)** and **Azure and other cloud services +43%**. Satya Nadella, in the release: "Azure revenue surpassed $100 billion for the first time" and "Microsoft 365 Copilot reached over 30 million paid seats."

**Size, as of 2026-09-16.** Market capitalization **~$3.67 trillion** (Reserve `dtf/exposure`), share price **$493.73**, TTM revenue **$331.84B**, P/E **~28** (stockanalysis.com).

**Role in AI.** Microsoft is simultaneously a **major investor in OpenAI**, the **cloud that hosts much of its workload**, an **IP licensee**, and the **distributor** of AI into enterprise software through Copilot — and, since 2026, a model developer in its own right through its MAI models. Its FY2026 10-K states it plainly: "We have a long-term strategic partnership with OpenAI… **Microsoft is a major investor in OpenAI and will continue to receive revenue-sharing payments. We hold rights to OpenAI's intellectual property, including models and infrastructure, for integration into our products.**" On **2026-04-27** Microsoft published amended terms: **Microsoft no longer pays revenue share to OpenAI; revenue share from OpenAI to Microsoft continues through 2030 subject to a total cap; the IP license runs through 2032 and becomes non-exclusive; and OpenAI products ship first on Azure "unless Microsoft cannot and chooses not to support the necessary capabilities," with OpenAI free to distribute across all clouds.** **The stake percentage, its valuation and the size of OpenAI's Azure commitment are third-party reported figures, not company disclosures, and are not stated here.**

**Growth drivers.** Azure past $100B of annual revenue and growing 43% in the June quarter, with management describing demand as running ahead of supply; Copilot at more than 30 million paid Microsoft 365 seats; and a three-way model hedge — the OpenAI relationship, Anthropic's Claude embedded inside Copilot, and Microsoft's own MAI models (the Anthropic and MAI details are reported, not filing-sourced).

**Company-specific bear case.** **The capital-return question is aimed more squarely at Microsoft than at any other name in the basket.** Morningstar, summarizing the market's concern about Microsoft's AI spending in 2026: "With Microsoft, there is an extra fear that all of the capex they are investing to build AI data centers may not earn a high enough return to justify the gigantic outlays." The scale is in the 10-K: **additions to property and equipment of $115,948M in FY2026, against $64,551M in FY2025 and $44,477M in FY2024**, depreciated over useful lives the filing gives as "servers and network equipment, two to six years". **Figures circulating for FY2027 capex are analyst estimates, not company guidance, and are not stated here.** On earnings quality, Microsoft publishes non-GAAP measures that explicitly "exclude net gains and losses from investments in OpenAI"; other income included **$6.5B of net gains in FY2026 against $4.8B of net losses in FY2025**, primarily the dilution gain from the OpenAI recapitalization, worth **$5.0B of net income and $0.67 of diluted EPS** in FY2026 against a $3.6B and $0.49 decrease the year before. **A company that builds a bespoke non-GAAP measure to strip a private-AI mark out of its own earnings is the strongest available evidence that headline multiples across these seven are not comparable.** Separately, the April 2026 amendment converts Microsoft from gatekeeper to preferred partner — OpenAI can now buy compute anywhere; More Personal Computing is shrinking; and capacity constraints cap near-term Azure revenue regardless of demand.

### 6.5 Amazon — `AMZN` · Retail, advertising and AWS · live weight ~13.6%

**What it does, by revenue segment.** Q2 2026 (quarter ended 2026-06-30, released 2026-07-30, from Amazon's own earnings page): consolidated net sales **$200,606M (+20%)** and consolidated operating income **$27,461M (+43%)**, split across three reportable segments — **North America $116,177M (57.9%)** earning $9,123M, **International $42,197M (21.0%)** earning $1,717M, and **AWS $42,232M (21.1%, +37%)** earning **$16,621M (+64%)**. Within revenue lines, **online stores were $70,432M**, **third-party seller services $46,780M** and **advertising services $19,809M (+26%)**. **The shape that matters: AWS is about a fifth of sales and about 61% of consolidated operating income.** FY2025 revenue was $716.9B (stockanalysis.com).

**Size, as of 2026-09-16.** Market capitalization **~$2.67 trillion** (Reserve `dtf/exposure`), share price **$247.53**, TTM revenue **$775.68B** — the largest revenue base of the seven — P/E **~20** (stockanalysis.com).

**Role in AI.** AWS is one of the handful of places frontier AI actually runs, and Amazon is **Anthropic's principal infrastructure partner and an investor in it**: the FY2025 10-K records investments in "convertible notes from Anthropic, PBC, including **$2.7 billion we invested in 2025**". Amazon is also one of only two companies in the basket with a credible merchant AI-silicon program, in Trainium, alongside Alphabet's TPU. **The larger commitment figures reported around the Amazon–Anthropic relationship are third-party reports and are not stated here.**

**Growth drivers.** AWS reaccelerated to **+37%**, described in Amazon's own summary as its fastest growth in eighteen quarters, with operating income up 64%. Advertising grew 26% — nearly twice the rate of online stores and the highest-margin consumer-facing line. Project Rainier, the jointly announced Trainium cluster serving Anthropic, is reported to run more than a million Trainium2 chips (reported by Amazon and Anthropic in their own announcements, summarized second-hand here).

**Company-specific bear case.** **Capital spending and free cash flow.** The FY2025 10-K states: "**Cash capital expenditures were $77.7 billion, and $128.3 billion in 2024 and 2025**… both of which we expect to increase in 2026." CNBC reported on 2026-07-30 that Amazon raised 2026 cash-capex guidance to about $220B, with Andy Jassy attributing part of the increase to higher memory costs and saying Amazon "won't have enough capacity to meet all the demand we have in 2026" — **reported, not read from a filing.** Trailing-twelve-month free cash flow was reported negative. **Earnings quality is the second issue:** the 10-K records that "the **net gain of $15.2 billion in 2025** is primarily from an upward adjustment for observable changes in price relating to our nonvoting preferred stock in Anthropic", alongside the filing's own warning that "valuations of private companies are inherently more complex due to the lack of readily available market data" — and the same mechanism was reported to dominate the Q2 2026 net-income line. **Amazon's stake in Anthropic and AWS's revenue from Anthropic are correlated exposures to one counterparty.** Amazon also shortened the useful life of a subset of its servers and networking equipment from six years to five, effective 2025-01-01, citing the pace of AI development — a change that raises depreciation. Retail margins are thin: North America and International together earned $10.8B on $158.4B of sales, about 6.8%, against AWS's ~39%. Guidance for Q3 2026 implied a marked deceleration (reported). And roughly 16,000 corporate roles were cut in an announcement dated 2026-01-28, after about 14,000 in October 2025 (reported by CNBC and Bloomberg).

### 6.6 Meta Platforms — `META` · Advertising, and AI with no cloud to resell · live weight ~15.6%

**What it does, by revenue segment.** Meta is, to a first approximation, **one advertising business**. In Q2 2026 (quarter ended 2026-06-30, released 2026-07-29, from Meta's investor-relations release) total revenue was **$60,801M**, up 28% year over year, of which **Family of Apps — Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp, Messenger and Threads — was $60,370M, or 99.3%**. Advertising is roughly **98% of the total**; "Family of Apps Other" (WhatsApp paid messaging and subscriptions) was about $1.0B but grew **73%**; **Reality Labs revenue was $431M** against an operating loss of **$(4,619)M**. Family of Apps operating income was **$23,394M**, total operating income **$18,775M** (a 31% margin), net income **$15,848M**, diluted EPS **$6.18**, and capital expenditures **$31,080M**. Family daily active people reached **3.60 billion**, up 3%, on a headcount of **75,472**, down 1% year over year. **Meta is by far the least diversified of the seven.**

**Size, as of 2026-09-16.** Market capitalization **~$1.72 trillion** (Reserve `dtf/exposure`), share price **$676.10**, TTM revenue **$228.25B**, TTM net income **$68.1B**, P/E **~25** (stockanalysis.com).

**Role in AI.** Meta has **no cloud to sell and no chips to sell**. Its AI case is two-part: AI making its own advertising system more efficient, and a bet that it can build frontier models itself. **Muse Spark**, launched **2026-04-08** as the first model from Meta Superintelligence Labs, is **closed-weights** — a break from the open-source Llama line — and Meta says it matches Llama 4 Maverick's capability at "an order of magnitude less compute", with API access planned for third-party developers (CNBC and TechCrunch, both 2026-04-08).

**Growth drivers.** Advertising grew **27%** in Q2 2026, with ad impressions up 14% and average price per ad up 12% (the advertising sub-line, the impression count and the pricing figures are all reported from investing.com's summary of Meta's Q2 slides; the release itself discloses Family of Apps revenue, not an advertising split). Mark Zuckerberg's line in the release: "AI is accelerating our core business today." WhatsApp monetization is compounding off a small base, and AI glasses are the only growing part of Reality Labs while Quest declines.

**Company-specific bear case.** Meta guided **FY2026 capital expenditures of $130–145 billion** in its Q2 2026 release of 2026-07-29 — note that its FY2025 10-K, filed 2026-01-29, had said "approximately $115 billion to $135 billion in 2026", so the range has been revised and the date on any capex number for Meta matters. **This is the sharpest form of the AI-return question in the basket: Meta spends hyperscaler money but, unlike Microsoft, Amazon and Alphabet, has no cloud through which to resell the capacity.** Reality Labs lost $4.6B in a single quarter on $431M of revenue. Legal exposure is now a headline item: Meta booked **$2.4B of legal charges in Q2 2026**, and on **2026-08-26** it settled the multi-state social-media-addiction litigation brought by 29 state attorneys general, with sweeping product changes. **Outlets disagree on the amount — CNBC reported $16.7 billion and the Washington Post reported $18 billion on the same day — so this document adopts neither figure**; Meta's own filing language would settle it. Reporting also put an expected ~$10B Q3 2026 legal accrual on the settlement, which was not read from a filing here. Finally, ~98% of revenue sits in one bucket exposed to privacy regulation, ad-market cycles and platform policy at once.

### 6.7 Tesla — `TSLA` · Vehicles, energy and physical AI · smallest by market capitalization · live weight ~14.0%

**What it does, by revenue segment.** Q2 2026 (quarter ended 2026-06-30, from Tesla's 10-Q filed 2026-07-23): total revenues **$28,236M** — **automotive $20,516M (72.7%)**, of which automotive sales $20,006M, **regulatory credits just $146M** and leasing $364M; **services & other $4,581M (16.2%)**; **energy generation and storage $3,139M (11.1%)**. Gross profit **$4,751M** (16.8% margin). R&D rose 49% to $2,371M and SG&A 45% to $1,982M, so **income from operations fell 57% to $398M — a 1.4% operating margin** — while net income was $1,128M. For the first half of 2026: revenue $50,623M, operating income $1,339M, **purchases of property and equipment $8,282M against $3,886M a year earlier**, and 22.3 GWh of energy storage deployed. Tesla also purchased **$2,002M of SpaceX equity** in the half.

**Size, as of 2026-09-16.** Market capitalization **~$1.43 trillion** (Reserve `dtf/exposure`) — the smallest of the seven — share price **~$362**, TTM revenue **$103.62B**, TTM net income **$3.81B**, P/E **~370** (stockanalysis.com). **Tesla is roughly one third of NVIDIA's revenue and about 27% of its market capitalization: it is the most expensive name in the basket on earnings by an order of magnitude.** Annual revenue was $96.77B in FY2023, $97.69B in FY2024 and **$94.83B in FY2025** — a decline — with net income falling from $15.0B to $7.1B to $3.8B across the same three years (stockanalysis.com).

**Role in AI.** Tesla's own framing, from the 10-Q's MD&A: "We are focused on bringing artificial intelligence into the real world, through products and services like FSD (Supervised) and Robotaxi, as well as working to develop and commercialize AI robots (including Optimus)." **Tesla is the basket's physical-AI exposure — the only constituent whose AI case is about vehicles and robots rather than data centers, and a buyer of AI compute that sells nothing into the AI stack.**

**Growth drivers.** The 10-Q confirms only that Tesla "continued to expand and refine our Robotaxi service after its June 2025 launch"; the city-by-city expansion, the move to unsupervised operation and Optimus V3 production timing are reported by Electrek, Basenor and Morningstar, not by the filing. The verifiable drivers are in the filing: 22.3 GWh of storage deployed in the half, services and other growing about 50% year over year at record gross profit, and **$4.05B of deferred FSD revenue** at 2026-06-30 to be recognized as features ship. Electrek's coverage of the same quarter reported a record 480,126 deliveries (reported, not in the filing extract). The milestones in the shareholder-approved pay package are disclosed in the 10-Q and double as management's stated ambition: **20 million vehicles delivered, 10 million active FSD subscriptions, 1 million bots delivered, 1 million robotaxis in commercial operation**, and adjusted-EBITDA tiers running from $50B to $400B — against trailing-twelve-month net income of $3.8B.

**Company-specific bear case.** **Profitability collapsed while revenue grew** — operating income down 57% on revenue up 25.5%, at a 1.4% margin. **Regulatory credits, which are close to pure margin, fell from $439M to $146M in the quarter and from $1,034M to $526M for the half**, a decline reported to follow the September 2025 expiry of the US federal EV tax credit. Capital spending more than doubled in the half. A P/E near 370 is pricing robotaxi and Optimus, not cars, and Morningstar attributed market disappointment in 2026 to a robotaxi rollout slower than the market had expected at the start of the year. China was **$20,962M, or 22.1%, of FY2025 revenue** — the largest China exposure in the basket. The $2.0B SpaceX purchase is a related-party transaction with another company controlled by Tesla's chief executive. **Equal weighting amplifies every line of this:** at a 14.29% target, MAG7 holds roughly **6.9× the Tesla exposure** an S&P 500 index fund does, and about **2.4×** the exposure a cap-weighted Magnificent Seven does (Tesla was ~2.07% of the S&P 500 and ~6.0% of a cap-weighted Magnificent Seven on 2026-09-16, slickcharts; both ratios are illustrative and approximate, and index weights move every day).

**Cross-cutting note on the constituents.** Read together — and this is *this document's* reading of the public filings above, not a Reserve claim, because no Reserve source ties the seven together — these seven are not seven independent businesses for this purpose. Four of them are spending on the same data-center build-out; three have had reported profits materially moved by marks on the same small set of private AI companies; one sells to the other four; and one now licenses its frontier models from another member of the basket. They also compete with each other directly — TPU and Trainium against NVIDIA, three clouds against one another, Meta and Alphabet for the same advertising dollar, Apple and Alphabet in phones and search even as Apple licenses Gemini. **Equal weighting resets the dollar split between them; it does not remove the factor they share.** That argument is made in full in Section 4, *How the seven fit together — one trade, not seven*, its consequences for construction are in Section 7, *Methodology: equal weight, seven names, two months*, and the risks it creates for a holder are in Section 13, *Risks (read this in full)*.

---

## 7. Methodology: equal weight, seven names, two months

This section describes how MAG7's basket is defined, weighted, and reset. It is the most MAG7-specific part of this document, and the part where precision matters most, because MAG7's methodology is **not** a variation on its Reserve siblings' methodology — it is a different kind of rule. The Reserve AI DTFs on BNB Smart Chain select constituents from a screen and weight them by market capitalization under a per-name cap. MAG7 does neither. Its rule is a **named, closed list of seven companies held in equal dollar weights**, and the entire published statement of that rule is one paragraph stored on the contract itself.

Every Reserve Index DTF carries a `mandate()` string on its own Folio contract. That string is the DTF's methodology statement of record: it is public, it is immutable except by governance, and it is what the operators of the DTF are nominally accountable to. MAG7's reads, in full — read directly from `0xcef8db49…c781` on Base on 2026-09-16, with the curly quotation marks reproduced as they are stored onchain:

> The Reserve Magnificent 7 DTF offers equal-weight exposure to seven of the largest publicly traded stocks, commonly known as the “Magnificent 7.” The basket comprises Alphabet (GOOGL), Amazon (AMZN), Apple (AAPL), Meta Platforms (META), Microsoft (MSFT), Nvidia (NVDA), and Tesla (TSLA) stocks in equal weights and rebalances every two months.

That is the whole published methodology. Everything below is an unpacking of those two sentences, plus the onchain facts they do not mention.

### 7.1 MAG7 does not track a third-party index

**MAG7 does not track, license, or replicate any third-party index.** Nothing in the mandate, in the contract, or in Reserve's published material for this product identifies an external index that MAG7 is contractually following. The basket is defined by the mandate's own sentence and maintained by the DTF's own governance process. As with PHOTON and every other Reserve Index DTF, this means composition is a **discretionary** matter in the hands of the parties operating the DTF — a conflict of interest disclosed in Section 15, and a governance question handled in Section 7.5 below.

There is, however, a nuance here that PHOTON did not have. PHOTON's theme (AI data-center photonics) has no widely accepted benchmark, so its market-cap-and-cap methodology was a design choice with nothing to be measured against. The Magnificent Seven does have a benchmark, and **that benchmark is equal-weighted**. So is the dominant ETF for the theme. MAG7 is therefore methodologically aligned with the reference version of its own theme, even though it is *un*aligned with its cap-weighted Reserve siblings.

| Vehicle | Weighting | Rebalance cadence | How the exposure is held |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Bloomberg Magnificent 7 Index** — the theme's reference benchmark | **Equal-dollar**, a fixed basket of seven | Quarterly (Mar / Jun / Sep / Dec), effective after the close on the second Wednesday | Index; not directly investable |
| **Roundhill Magnificent Seven ETF (MAGS)** — the closest live comparator | **Equal weight** | Quarterly, *"typically on the third Friday"* of those months (Roundhill's own wording, reported) | **Primarily total-return swaps and forwards**, plus Treasury bills and some direct shares; actively managed (structure per Roundhill's prospectus) |
| **S&P 500 Equal Weight Index** — the reference equal-weight construction | Equal, 0.2% per name | Quarterly | Index |
| **MAG7 (this DTF)** | **Equal weight, 1/7 ≈ 14.29% per name** | **Every two months — stated in the mandate; not enforced onchain (Section 7.4)** | **Physically held Coinbase tokenized stocks on Base**, reset by onchain Dutch auction |

Two details in that table are worth pulling out:

- **The reference index splits Alphabet; MAG7 does not.** Bloomberg's methodology states that where a company has multiple securities, its equal weight is split among them in proportion to free-float market capitalization — so the index holds both share classes of Alphabet. MAG7 holds **`GOOGLc`, Class A only**. It is a small divergence from the theme's own benchmark, but it is a real one, and it is verifiable from the basket (Section 5).
- **A US-registered fund cannot do what MAG7 does directly.** Roundhill states on its own product page that MAGS *"utilizes total return swaps in order to maintain compliance with RIC diversification tests."* A regulated US fund cannot simply hold seven names at one-seventh each; it needs a derivative overlay to get there. A DTF faces no RIC diversification test, so MAG7 holds the seven tokenized stocks outright at equal weight, with no swap counterparty — though holding the token is not holding the share (Section 9). That is a genuine structural difference, and it runs the other way from most comparisons in this document — see Section 2.1 for the ones that do not.

### 7.2 There is no selection screen — the seven are named in the mandate

PHOTON's constituents have to *qualify*: US-listed, tokenizable, identified as deriving significant revenue from AI photonics, above a minimum market capitalization. **MAG7 has no screen of any kind.** There is:

- **no market-capitalization minimum;**
- **no per-name cap** — at one-seventh each there is nothing for a cap to bind on;
- **no liquidity, listing, revenue, or sector test;**
- **no index-eligibility rule to pass, and nothing for a constituent to fail.**

The roster is simply the seven companies **named by ticker in the mandate**: Alphabet, Amazon, Apple, Meta Platforms, Microsoft, NVIDIA, and Tesla. That is the selection rule in its entirety. It is a closed list, not a screen, and the honest way to describe it is that MAG7 is a **named basket that is rebalanced**, not a rules-based basket that is reconstituted.

Two consequences follow, and both are open ends rather than features:

1. **There is no published rule for what happens if the list has to change.** If one of the seven were acquired, delisted, split into successor entities, or simply stopped being described as a Magnificent Seven name, nothing in the mandate, in the contract, or in Reserve's published material says what MAG7 would do. PHOTON at least has a stated fallback — a constituent that no longer meets the criteria is removed at the next rebalance. MAG7 has **no criteria to fail**, so any such change would be a discretionary decision, taken by the DTF's governance and executed as the transaction described in Section 7.5. Treat the seven names as the current composition, not a permanent guarantee.
2. **The binding practical constraint sits one layer down, at the issuer.** For a name to be in the basket at all, it must exist as a Coinbase tokenized stock on Base. That is why the basket was built out in stages over August and September 2026 rather than arriving complete (Section 7.4), and it is why the relevant list to check is the one Coinbase publishes, not an index provider's. Section 9 covers the issuer layer in full — including the separate point that the issuer's own prospectus leaves delistings and corporate actions to open-ended "Adjustment Event" discretion. The two gaps stack: neither layer publishes a rule.

### 7.3 Equal weight, and what it actually does

**How it is implemented.** At each rebalance, the governance transaction encodes a **target quantity of each underlying token per MAG7 share** — not a target percentage. Those quantities are chosen so that each of the seven legs is worth approximately one-seventh of net asset value **at the price snapshot used when the rebalance was proposed**. Between rebalances the quantities are fixed, so the weights drift with prices. MAG7 is what the protocol calls a **tracking** DTF: `rebalanceControl().weightControl` is `false`, which means the operator opening the auctions **cannot move the basket weights** during execution — it may only narrow each token's price band inside the range set when the rebalance was proposed. Section 8 covers that machinery.

**"Equal weight" is therefore exact in intent and approximate in the encoding.** The targets written into the most recent rebalance (nonce 3, 2026-09-09), valued at that rebalance's own embedded mid prices, were not exactly one-seventh: they ran from 14.15% to 14.76% against a 14.2857% target, a spread of 0.61 percentage points. No published off-chain weighting procedure for MAG7 was found for this document, so why the encoded legs are not exactly equal at the rebalance's own band mids is not established. Live weights drift further as prices move.

| Constituent token | Encoded target at rebalance 3 (2026-09-09) | Live weight (2026-09-16) |
|---|---|---|
| AAPLc | 14.76% | 14.87% |
| GOOGLc | 14.37% | 14.48% |
| NVDAc | 14.22% | 13.42% |
| TSLAc | 14.18% | 13.98% |
| AMZNc | 14.17% | 13.57% |
| MSFTc | 14.16% | 14.13% |
| METAc | 14.15% | 15.55% |
| **Spread, largest − smallest** | **0.61 pp** | **2.13 pp** |

*Illustrative and approximate. The target column is computed for this document from the rebalance transaction's own encoded price bands; the live column is the Reserve API read (`api.reserve.org/current/dtf`) used throughout this document, as of 2026-09-16 at approximately 15:25 UTC. Both move — the live column moves continuously, and a later read the same day, at 16:30 UTC, gives a 2.09 pp spread. Read the current weights on app.reserve.org.*

**What equal weighting changes, relative to cap weighting the same seven names.** This is the clearest single statement of what the methodology decision buys and costs. The figures below are computed for this document from the constituent market capitalizations in Reserve's own `dtf/exposure` data, read 2026-09-16, and are illustrative and approximate; third-party aggregators put the aggregate at $23.7–23.8T. They are a snapshot of relative size, not a forecast of anything.

| Name | Market cap | Weight if cap-weighted | MAG7's target weight | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| NVIDIA | ~$5.21T | 21.92% | 14.29% | −7.63 pp |
| Apple | ~$4.86T | 20.41% | 14.29% | −6.12 pp |
| Alphabet | ~$4.23T | 17.77% | 14.29% | −3.48 pp |
| Microsoft | ~$3.67T | 15.42% | 14.29% | −1.13 pp |
| Amazon | ~$2.67T | 11.22% | 14.29% | +3.07 pp |
| Meta Platforms | ~$1.72T | 7.25% | 14.29% | +7.04 pp |
| Tesla | ~$1.43T | 6.01% | 14.29% | +8.28 pp |

*Alphabet's market capitalization above is the company total, and sources differ on whether the GOOG class is included: the spread between sources for this name is about 0.9%, larger than for any of the other six. MAG7 holds the `GOOGLc`, Class A token only (Section 7.1).*

Under cap weighting the largest position would be about **3.6×** the smallest. Under equal weighting the ratio is 1.00× at each reset. But the aggregate diversification gain is modest and should not be oversold: on a Herfindahl basis, equal weighting takes the basket from roughly **six effective names to seven**, because the seven companies' market caps already sit inside one order of magnitude of each other. **The big difference is per-name, not in aggregate.** Converting a cap-weighted version of this basket to equal weight today would require roughly **18.4%** one-way turnover.

**The arguments for equal-weighting this particular basket:**

- It caps single-name risk by construction. No constituent reaches the ~22% that NVIDIA would carry under cap weighting, and no constituent can grow into a dominant position between resets without being trimmed at the next one.
- It matches the theme's reference benchmark and its dominant ETF (Section 7.1), so "Magnificent 7" means the same thing in this product as it does on a terminal.
- It is **mechanically contrarian**: at each reset it sells what has risen relative to the group and buys what has lagged, with no discretion at the weighting step. S&P Dow Jones Indices describes exactly this effect for its own equal-weight index — *"it systematically tilts away from stocks that have risen the most and tilts toward those that have fallen behind, imparting an anti-momentum or contrarian bias."*
- Leadership inside the seven rotates, and equal weighting does not have to guess which of them leads next.

**The arguments against, which are stronger than they are usually made to sound:**

- **The documented equal-weight premium comes mostly from somewhere this basket cannot reach.** S&P Dow Jones Indices attributes **more than half** of its equal-weight index's historical excess return to holding more of the index's *smaller* constituents, and the academic work attributes the rest to short-term reversal and idiosyncratic volatility — effects that fall with size, price and liquidity. A seven-name basket of mega-caps has no smaller constituents to tilt toward and sits in the corner of the market where those effects are smallest. The mechanism may still operate directionally; **its magnitude should not be assumed to transfer**, and anyone citing the S&P 500 equal-weight literature in a MAG7 context is importing a result whose principal driver does not exist here.
- **It is structurally anti-momentum, which makes it a bet on mean reversion rather than a free lunch.** That is the contrarian bullet above read as a cost: whenever the largest names in a group keep leading it, an equal-weight rule trims them into that leadership and tops up the laggards, and cap weighting does better as a result. Commentators have made exactly that argument about mega-cap technology in recent years; this document quotes no comparative performance figures for it, or for anything else (Section 12.4).
- **It is a deliberate deviation from the market's own weighting.** A cap-weighted basket says: hold these seven in the proportions the market holds them. An equal-weighted one is an **active view** that the market's relative pricing of the seven is wrong. Read it as a view, not as neutrality.
- **Higher turnover, and therefore higher cost.** Equal weighting requires a trade at every reset; cap weighting largely maintains itself. In Plyakha, Uppal and Vilkov's sample, equal weighting's turnover ran roughly six times the value-weighted portfolio's on a measure that counts both purchases and sales. What that costs MAG7 specifically is auction execution plus the protocol fees in Section 12, not an institutional equity desk's commission.
- **It does nothing about the basket's common risk.** Equal weighting reduces *single-name* concentration. It does not reduce the fact that all seven are US mega-caps exposed to the same AI capital-expenditure cycle — see Section 4 and Section 13.2. **Seven equal slices of one factor is still one factor.**

### 7.4 The rebalance cadence: the mandate's intention, and the onchain record

**What the mandate says.** The basket *"rebalances every two months."* Taken as arithmetic, that is **six resets a year against the quarterly four** used by every comparable vehicle verified for this document — 50% more rebalance events. The cadence, not the equal weighting, is MAG7's genuine methodological outlier.

**What the contract does.** Nothing onchain enforces, schedules, or measures a cadence. There is **no timer, no keeper, no deadline and no penalty** in the Folio contract. A rebalance happens when and only when a governance transaction calls `startRebalance`, and the two-month figure exists solely as a sentence inside the `mandate()` string. **Never read "every two months" as an observed or guaranteed schedule.** It is a stated intention.

**The onchain record, stated neutrally.** Three rebalances have run since the contract was deployed on 2026-08-04 (all times UTC, read from the chain on 2026-09-16):

| Rebalance | Opened | Auction window granted | Auctions opened |
|---|---|---|---|
| nonce 1 | 2026-08-20 12:23:47 | 48 hours | 1 (2026-08-20, 12:25:03 → 12:40:03) |
| nonce 2 | 2026-09-06 23:16:13 | 24 hours | **0** — the window lapsed and the rebalance expired without moving anything |
| nonce 3 | 2026-09-09 18:35:15 | 48 hours | 3, all within one hour (18:40:43 → 19:30:41) |

Four auctions in total; `nextAuctionId()` reads 4. The gaps between rebalances were **17 days and then 3 days**. **No two-month interval has ever elapsed between MAG7 rebalances.** The record spans six weeks, and it covers the period in which the DTF was built out from a 100%-USDC basket — it held no stock exposure at all until **2026-08-20** — into the seven-name basket the mandate describes: MAG7 became a seven-name basket during rebalance 3's first auction, at **18:48:43 UTC on 2026-09-09**. It is too short and too atypical to be evidence of any cadence, in either direction. It is not a two-month schedule and it is not a departure from one. It is a build-out.

One disclosure that belongs with the table: **the rebalance and auction counts above come from the chain, and a reader checking the Reserve app will see fewer auctions than this.** This document uses the chain. See Section 15.

**What the cadence costs.** More resets mean more execution cost, and every argument in Section 7.3's second list applies with more force at a higher frequency. The academic evidence directionally favors a shorter rebalancing interval, but the published work reports one-, six- and twelve-month intervals — **there is no two-month figure, and interpolating one would be an invention.** The honest summary is that **the cadence is a defensible choice with a real cost attached, not a free improvement.**

### 7.5 Who can change the basket, and how fast

This is the part of MAG7's methodology that is easiest to miss, and the part an investor should read most carefully. **The mandate is a string. The basket is whatever the most recent `startRebalance` transaction encoded.** The two can, in principle, diverge.

The mechanics, all read from the chain on 2026-09-16:

- MAG7's optimistic selector registry (`0xA5977360e7bd8EF6dB8992F1Dc68B5415995Fa75`) allows **exactly one function on exactly one target**: the selector `0x207c8eed` — `startRebalance` — on the MAG7 Folio. Nothing else can travel the fast path: no fee change, no role change, no upgrade, no mandate edit.
- But `startRebalance` takes the **entire token list and every weight as its arguments**. The caller supplies the basket. So the single thing the fast path can do is **define what MAG7 holds**.
- The fast path is a **4-hour veto delay plus a 20-hour veto window** — 24 hours in total — after which the proposal is executable immediately, **with no timelock queue**. Stopping it requires holders of at least **1% of vote-locked RSR at the proposal's snapshot** to vote against it inside that window. Fast-path proposals can be made by either of two Safes holding the timelock's optimistic-proposer role — `0xF770497B…` (two-of-five) and `0xb3CF59A5…` (three-of-six) — each throttled to three proposals per twelve-hour window. Every rebalance to date was proposed by `0xF770497B…`.
- This is not hypothetical. Rebalance 1 encoded five entries (USDC plus four of the stocks); rebalances 2 and 3 encoded eight (USDC plus all seven). **TSLAc, MSFTc and AMZNc entered MAG7's basket through optimistic proposals**, on that 24-hour clock.

**Stated plainly: a constituent can be added to or removed from MAG7 by a multisig on a 24-hour clock, unless at least 1% of vote-locked RSR vetoes it.** Everything else about the DTF — fees, roles, contract upgrades — runs the slow path instead: roughly a 2-day voting delay, a 3-day vote, and a 2-day timelock, about a week in total, with a 10% quorum. Section 8.10 sets out the full configuration and Section 13.8 treats it as a risk.

Three constraints do apply once a rebalance is running, and they are real:

- **Weights cannot be changed during execution.** `weightControl = false` fixes the quantities at proposal time; the auction operator may only narrow prices inside the band already set (`priceControl = PARTIAL`).
- **Price bands have been set narrowly relative to what the protocol allows.** On the most recent rebalance each token's band ran from one quarter to four times its mid price — a 16× range against a protocol ceiling of 100× — and each auction runs 15 minutes with a maximum size of $1,000,000 of notional per token, a cap that has never come close to binding at MAG7's size.
- **Bidding into the auctions is permissionless, but opening them has not been.** On all three rebalances to date the operator-exclusive window was set equal to the full lifetime of the rebalance, so in practice **only the two auction-launcher Safes have ever been able to open an auction**; the public never could.

### 7.6 What this means for an investor

MAG7's methodology is the easiest to state of any Reserve Index DTF this document compares it against, and the least constrained by a screen. Seven companies, named; equal dollars across them; a stated two-month reset. If you want exposure to the Magnificent Seven as a group, in equal proportions, in one token, that is exactly what this is — and it matches how the theme's own reference benchmark is built.

Four things to carry away:

1. **You are buying a rule about dollars, not a rule about companies.** The companies are fixed by name in the mandate. The methodology only decides how the money is split between them, and it splits it evenly. There is no screen, no cap, and nothing for a constituent to qualify for or fail.
2. **Equal weight is an active view, and a specific one.** Relative to owning the same seven at market weights, MAG7 is systematically underweight the largest names and overweight the smallest, and it re-imposes that stance at every reset. Held for long enough, that is a bet that the seven mean-revert against one another. It is a defensible bet. It is not a neutral one, and the strongest evidence usually cited for it comes from a part of the market these seven are not in.
3. **Two months is an intention, not a schedule.** The mandate says every two months; nothing onchain enforces it; three rebalances have run, on 2026-08-20, 2026-09-06 and 2026-09-09, as the basket was built out. Judge the cadence from the onchain record as it accumulates, not from the sentence.
4. **Composition is a governance outcome, not a code guarantee.** The basket is whatever the last rebalance transaction encoded, and a new one can be proposed and executed in 24 hours on the fast path. Before relying on the seven names and weights in this document, read the live basket on app.reserve.org — and carry the three caveats set out in Section 0 and Section 13.18.

---

## 8. What a DTF is (the Reserve Index Protocol), end to end

MAG7 is built with Reserve's **Index Protocol**. This section explains the wrapper in depth — what a DTF is, how it is priced, how minting and redeeming work, how it rebalances, how fees flow, how it is governed, how it is secured, and how a person participates in its governance. If you understand this section you understand the machinery underneath every Reserve Index DTF, not only MAG7. Everything MAG7-specific in this section was read from the Base chain, from Reserve's live documentation, or from the deployed contract source on **2026-09-16**, except where a sentence names Reserve's indexer or app as its source, and every parameter here can be changed by governance — reconfirm onchain before relying on it.

### 8.1 Definition: DTF, Index DTF, and "RToken"

A **DTF** is a **Decentralized Token Fund**. (DTFs were originally called **Decentralized Token Folios** — you will still see "Folio" in older Reserve materials and in the protocol's own contract names, including MAG7's — but the current name is **Fund**; the two refer to the same thing.) A DTF is a **fully asset-backed ERC-20 token** created with Reserve's open-source smart contracts that represents a basket of underlying tokens held onchain. Anyone can mint, redeem and govern one permissionlessly.

Reserve has **two families** of DTF:

- **Index DTFs** — Reserve's **Index Protocol**. They efficiently manage diversified portfolios of tens to hundreds of tokens, are lightweight (no collateral plugins and no price oracles inside the contract), and instead of paying yield they charge **mint and TVL (management) fees**. RSR's role here is **vote-locking for governance**. **MAG7 is an Index DTF.**
- **Yield DTFs** — Reserve's separate **Yield Protocol**. They hold yield-bearing collateral, and RSR stakers provide first-loss overcollateralization that is slashable on collateral default, in exchange for a share of yield. *MAG7 is not a Yield DTF; Yield DTFs are described here only for context, and the distinction matters in Section 8.9.*

You will also see the term **"RToken"** in the Reserve app, in videos and in the community. RToken is the older technical name for *any* token launched on Reserve, Yield or Index. "MAG7 is an Index DTF" and "MAG7 is an Index RToken" mean the same thing.

Reserve's docs describe the Index Protocol as "a lightweight framework for wrapping handfuls to hundreds of ERC-20 tokens into single fungible assets," with permissionless NAV-based issuance, a broad asset universe, Dutch-auction rebalancing with onchain price discovery, continuous fees accruing in DTF shares, and custom onchain governance. The same docs summarize Index DTFs as "like ETFs, but decentralized: they're permissionless, composable, and live 24/7 onchain" — and note that a DTF on Base can package **100+ assets**. MAG7 uses seven of them.

Two practical limits worth knowing: the Folio contract cannot hold a non-standard ERC-20 (fee-on-transfer tokens, ERC-777 / callback tokens, tokens with no `decimals()` or with multiple entry points must be wrapped first), and Reserve's own app does **not yet support permissionless creation of Index DTFs** — the deployment UI is, in Reserve's words, "still under construction." Neither limit affects holding MAG7.

### 8.2 Asset-backed, not algorithmic

A crucial property: **DTFs are fully asset-backed 1:1 with exogenous collateral** — external, unrelated assets — that can be redeemed at any time, onchain, for the underlying. Reserve's own FAQ puts it directly: DTFs "are not algorithmically backed," and "do not have the recursive, negative feedback loops found in certain algorithmic stablecoins because DTFs are not minted from self-referential endogenous collateral."

For MAG7 specifically, every MAG7 token is backed by, and redeemable for, a defined set of **Coinbase Tokenized Stocks on Base** — the seven `<TICKER>c` tokens listed in Section 5. As of 2026-09-16 the Folio's `totalAssets()` returns exactly those seven tokens and nothing else; the cash leg went to zero on 2026-09-09.

**What that backing is, and what it is not, is Section 9, and you should read it before you rely on the word "backed."** The DTF layer's promise is precise and narrow: MAG7 is redeemable onchain, at any time, for the seven tokenized-stock tokens themselves. It is **not** a promise that you can redeem those tokens for shares — under the issuer's prospectus a secondary-market holder is an **Unvested Holder** with no redemption right to the underlying shares. Two different layers, two different guarantees.

### 8.3 Pricing: NAV

A DTF's value is based on **Net Asset Value (NAV)** — the combined value of the underlying tokens in the basket, divided across the shares. The Folio contract itself **holds no price oracle**: it tracks balances, and the one-to-many exchange rate is exposed onchain by `toAssets(uint256 shares, Math.Rounding rounding)`, which returns exactly which underlying tokens, and how much of each, a given quantity of shares is worth. `totalAssets()` returns the same thing for the whole basket.

Because anyone can read that function and anyone can mint or redeem against it, arbitrage keeps the market price of the token close to the value of its underlying basket. The dollar figures you see on app.reserve.org and in Reserve's API are computed **offchain** from third-party price feeds — for MAG7 the API reports its price sources as "chainlink, zapper, alchemy, defillama" — and they inherit whatever staleness those feeds carry. That matters more for MAG7 than for a crypto-asset DTF, because the underlying equity marks are 24/5 and pause outside market hours (Section 9).

One rounding detail integrators should know: use `Math.Rounding.Ceil` when computing the basket needed for a mint and `Math.Rounding.Floor` when computing the proceeds of a redemption, use the asset order the call returns, and expect the amounts to move slightly downward once a day as the TVL fee accrues (Section 8.6).

### 8.4 Minting and redeeming (the heart of the design)

Minting and redeeming are what tie a DTF to its NAV. **Anyone can mint or redeem permissionlessly** — there are no authorized participants and no gatekeepers at the DTF layer, unlike an ETF.

- **Mint:** deposit the underlying basket tokens — or, via the zapper, a single token of your choice — and receive newly created MAG7 at NAV, less the mint fee.
- **Redeem:** burn MAG7 and receive the seven underlying tokens back at NAV, pro rata.

This happens in **three ways**, in increasing order of sophistication:

1. **Zapper (one-step, the default in the app).** You choose a single token to spend or receive, and the app handles the swaps and the mint or redeem in one atomic transaction. On Base the zapper's picker carries several hundred curated Base tokens — USDC, WETH, cbBTC, bridged USDT, EURC, AERO and RSR among them, with the live count, the endpoint and the date in Section 10.8; gas is always paid in ETH. Reserve's docs warn that zaps commonly leave "dust amounts… on the order of 1–10 basis points of the input value."
2. **Manual mint/redeem.** Switch to manual mode and deposit or receive the exact per-token basket amounts, with precise slippage control. Useful if you already hold, or want, the seven underlying tokens.
3. **Direct contract call.** `mint(shares, receiver, minSharesOut)` and `redeem(shares, receiver, assets, minAmountsOut)` on the Folio proxy. This is the escape hatch that ensures you are never dependent on any front-end or offchain service to exit a position.

**The MAG7-specific nuance, and it is the opposite of the one in Reserve's BNB Smart Chain AI DTFs.** Reserve's docs describe a different arrangement for DTFs whose underlying tokens have their main liquidity offchain: the zapper leans on **RFQ / intent systems**, where approved minters source the basket and act as the trading layer — the pattern Reserve's published reference documents describe for its BNB Smart Chain AI DTFs. **MAG7 does not work that way today.** Its underlying c-tokens are routable onchain on Base, and a live quote on 2026-09-16 (100 USDC → MAG7) returned a single atomic route with no dust and no intent layer. A second consequence follows and is worth stating plainly: **buying MAG7 through the app mints new shares rather than trading the Aerodrome pool** — forcing the trade path and forcing the mint path returned answers agreeing to eight significant figures on the same date. Selling is the constrained side, because a sale has to find liquidity. Section 10 covers venues, depth and the practical sizing question.

Two conventions that trip integrators: the zapper's `slippage` parameter is an **inverse fraction** (`200` means 1/200 = 0.5%), and a zapper quote is wallet-specific and time-sensitive — never reuse calldata for a different signer.

### 8.5 Rebalancing via onchain Dutch auctions

When the basket needs to change, the change is executed through **onchain Dutch auctions**, not by a manager trading at a desk. The v5 lifecycle has four steps:

1. **Start the rebalance.** Only the `REBALANCE_MANAGER` may call `startRebalance(...)`, supplying, per token, a weight range `{low, spot, high}`, a price range `{low, high}` and a `maxAuctionSize`; basket-unit limits `{low, spot, high}`; and two durations — `auctionLauncherWindow`, the length of the auction-launcher's exclusive window, and `ttl`, the rebalance's life. The contract stores them as `restrictedUntil = startedAt + auctionLauncherWindow` and `availableUntil = startedAt + ttl`. The protocol caps a rebalance's life at **4 weeks** and each token's price range at **100×**.
2. **The launcher window.** During the restricted period **only** an `AUCTION_LAUNCHER` can call `openAuction(...)`. Inside the governance-set bounds it may narrow limits and narrow prices; it can **never** lower a price below the approved band, change the sell/buy tokens, or extend the rebalance's expiry.
3. **The permissionless window.** After `restrictedUntil` and before `availableUntil`, **anyone** can call `openAuctionUnrestricted(nonce)` with the pre-approved parameters, provided the rebalance was started in priced mode. Reserve's own contract comments say this window "exists primarily to avoid strong reliance on the `AUCTION_LAUNCHER`."
4. **Bidding and settlement.** **Anyone** can bid while permissionless bidding is enabled. Each auction runs a declining exponential price curve `P(t) = startPrice × exp(−k·t)` from its start price to its end price, with a **30-second warm-up** before the first bid so that competition starts in the first block, and all eligible surplus/deficit token pairs run **in parallel**. Every fill updates the basket atomically.

**How MAG7 is configured, as deployed:** `auctionLength()` is **900 seconds — 15 minutes**; `bidsEnabled()` is **true**; `rebalanceControl()` decodes to **`weightControl = false`** and **`priceControl = PARTIAL`**. That combination makes MAG7 a **"tracking" DTF**: the basket quantities are fixed when the rebalance is *proposed*, not when the auction runs, and the launcher may only narrow prices inside the band set at `startRebalance`. In the most recent rebalance each token's price band was **mid ÷ 4 to mid × 4** — a deliberately narrow 16× band against the protocol's 100× ceiling — and `maxAuctionSize` was set to **$1,000,000 of notional per token** at that rebalance's own mid prices, a cap far above anything MAG7's size has needed.

**The observed record.** Three rebalances and four auctions have run, each auction the full 15 minutes; the middle rebalance expired having opened none. As of 2026-09-16 no rebalance is open — note that `getRebalance()` still returns non-zero data, which the contract's own comment warns does not imply an active rebalance, so check `availableUntil`. Section 7, *Methodology: equal weight, seven names, two months*, has the record, the dates and the cadence question — the two-month interval is a sentence in `mandate()` that nothing onchain enforces, times or triggers.

**One structural fact about those windows.** On all three rebalances to date `restrictedUntil` was set **equal to** `availableUntil`, and where those two timestamps coincide there is no permissionless window at all — so in practice **only the two `AUCTION_LAUNCHER` Safes have ever been able to open a MAG7 auction.** Bidding has been open to anyone throughout.

**The trusted-filler path, described as a mechanism.** Alongside direct bids, a Folio can settle auction flow through **Trusted Fillers** — an opt-in framework for *async* swaps with known fillers, which sit outside the atomic bid path and are constrained by the Folio through EIP-1271 signature checks. `createTrustedFill(...)` is permissionless and deploys a fresh filler contract per fill; an admin can claw back an in-flight fill with `emergencyCloseTrustedFill(...)`. MAG7 has the framework switched on (`trustedFillerEnabled() = true`, registry `0x72DB5f49D0599C314E2f2FEDf6Fe33E1bA6C7A18`). **This document does not identify which filler implementation, if any, is registered for MAG7 on Base, and does not name a solver network behind its auctions.** The registry exposes no enumeration function, only `isAllowed(address)`; Reserve's indexer records **26 bids across the four auctions from 18 distinct bidder addresses, each bid settled through a separate one-off filler contract**, and none of those parties is publicly identified. Reserve's published trusted-filler repository describes a CoW Swap filler as the framework's implemented filler at the protocol level — that is a statement about the framework, not an attribution for MAG7's auctions.

### 8.6 Fees and protocol revenue

Index DTFs do not earn from yield-bearing collateral. They generate revenue from **two fee streams**, both collected in the form of the DTF token itself:

| Fee | Basis | Protocol ceiling | How it accrues |
|---|---|---|---|
| **TVL (management) fee** | Basket NAV | **10% annualized** (`MAX_TVL_FEE`) | New DTF shares minted **once every 24 hours** (a compound accrual, applied daily since protocol release 4.0.0 — *not* block-by-block) |
| **Mint fee** | Each new issuance | **5%** (`MAX_MINT_FEE`, floor 0.03% when non-zero) | Deducted from each mint; causes no supply inflation |

**For MAG7 the fees are a 0.30% mint fee and a 0.6% annual TVL fee** — read directly from `mintFee()` and `tvlFee()` on 2026-09-16, and far below the ceilings. (A note on precision: the governance input was exactly 0.6% per year, but the per-second value the contract stores is rounded down, so the rate the contract actually realizes sits a fraction below 0.6% — right to twelve significant figures, and not worth calling exact.) Reserve's docs' Index-DTF overview page shows lower ceilings, "< 5% APY" and "< 1%" — the contract constants are authoritative at **10% and 5%**.

A **platform fee** is taken by the protocol out of both streams *before* the remainder reaches the DTF's own recipients. For MAG7 the live `FolioDAOFeeRegistry` returns the protocol default — **33.3333%**, with a **0.10% annual fee floor** — and MAG7 carries no per-DTF override. One quotable consequence of MAG7's own numbers: at a 0.30% mint fee the DAO's floor claim (0.10%) and its one-third share (0.10%) **coincide exactly**.

After the platform share, MAG7's remainder goes to exactly two onchain recipients. See Section 12, *Fees and costs*, for that split, for where every fee dollar lands, for why the chain's 30/70 and the app's 20.00/46.67 are the same numbers in different bases, and for the Coinbase layer's own fees, which sit *beneath* the DTF and are borne by the basket rather than billed to you.

Where the platform's share goes: Reserve's docs describe protocol fees being swept into a contract that **automatically market-buys RSR and sends it to the burn address**, a deflationary mechanism applied across every Index DTF regardless of which governance token the fund uses.

Two mechanics worth carrying: accrued fees sit as **pending fee shares** until someone calls `distributeFees()` — a permissionless call, surfaced in the app as a "Distribute Fees" button, with roughly **2.4 MAG7** pending as of 2026-09-16 (illustrative and approximate) — and because the TVL fee is an accrual against NAV, **the longer you hold, the more of it you pay**, exactly as with a traditional fund's expense ratio, just computed onchain.

### 8.7 Governance and roles

Each Index DTF behaves like its own miniature protocol. The deployer picks an ERC-20 for **vote-locking** — **RSR by default, and RSR for MAG7** — and holders of that locked token steer the DTF through onchain proposals. Reserve's **Optimistic Governor** provides a dual-path model:

- **Standard path** — for everything high-impact: fees, fee recipients, basket membership, auction length, roles, the trusted-filler registry, the proxy upgrade. Propose → voting delay → vote → queue in the timelock → execute, entirely onchain.
- **Optimistic path** — for pre-allowlisted actions. These skip affirmative voting and execute after a short **veto window** unless enough voting power votes against; during that window **only `Against` votes are accepted** ("silence is consent"). If the veto threshold is met, the proposal is defeated and the governor **automatically opens a standard confirmation proposal**; votes do not carry over. For MAG7 the allowlist holds exactly one selector, `startRebalance`, and because that call supplies the entire token set, the fast path is not merely administrative: **it is the power to change what MAG7 holds.** Section 7 states what that means for the basket; Section 8.10 gives the clock, the threshold and the registry entry.

Five safeguards fence the fast path: an allowlist of `(target, selector)` pairs that only slow governance can extend; permanently untouchable targets (the governor, the timelock, the selector registry and the staking vault can never be fast-path targets); a per-proposer rate limit; a shared Guardian that can cancel any non-final fast proposal; and the veto itself.

Governance is exercised through **scope-limited roles**, each, in Reserve's words, "sandboxed to the smallest set of function calls and parameter ranges needed for its mandate":

- **Admin** (`DEFAULT_ADMIN_ROLE`) — sets fees and fee recipients, sets auction length, adds or removes basket assets directly, sets the mandate and name, configures the trusted-filler registry and permissionless bidding, assigns every role, and owns the proxy admin that can upgrade the implementation. Gated by a timelock and by hard ceilings it cannot exceed.
- **Rebalance Manager** (`REBALANCE_MANAGER`) — the only role that can call `startRebalance`. Ending a rebalance is wider: `endRebalance()` can be called by the Admin, the Rebalance Manager or an Auction Launcher. The Rebalance Manager cannot touch fees or governance. *(This role was called `AUCTION_APPROVER` before protocol release 4.0.0; that name is stale for a v5 DTF like MAG7 and survives in the contract constants only as a deprecated hash.)*
- **Auction Launcher** (`AUCTION_LAUNCHER`) — opens and closes auctions inside the approved bands, and may refine prices and limits within them. It cannot change the tokens or extend the rebalance, but it can end one: `endRebalance()` is open to the Admin, the Rebalance Manager and either Auction Launcher alike. A DTF is not *required* to have one, since auctions can be opened permissionlessly where the windows allow it.
- **Optimistic Proposer** — creates fast-path proposals for allowlisted actions, subject to a throttle; revocable by a Guardian without a vote.
- **Brand Manager** (`BRAND_MANAGER`) — display metadata only. The deployed Folio source is blunt: this role "does not have any permissions. It is for off-chain use."
- **Guardian** — holds `CANCELLER_ROLE` on the timelock and can veto queued standard or optimistic proposals and revoke a compromised Optimistic Proposer; it cannot propose or execute anything itself.

Every proposal, vote and execution is recorded onchain. MAG7's own role holders, governor addresses and parameters are in Section 8.10, and they are also rendered on the DTF's **Details + Roles** and **Governance** pages in the Reserve app.

### 8.8 Security and audits

**What is published.** Reserve's security page for Index DTFs lists four third-party reviews of the Index protocol: **Trust Security** (Dec 2024), a **Cantina** competition (Jan 2025), **Trail of Bits** (Apr 2025) and **Pashov** (Jun 2025). The protocol repository's `audits/` directory in fact holds **five** reports — it adds a second Trail of Bits review, of version **4.0.0**, dated Jun 2025 — so the honest summary is that Trail of Bits reviewed both 2.0.0 and 4.0.0. Reserve also runs a **$10,000,000 bug bounty** with Cantina, and describes Trust Security as its standing security partner, which "reviews every piece of Solidity code before it is deployed onchain to be used in production."

**What is not published, and it must be said plainly.** MAG7 runs **Folio version 5.0.0**, released on **2025-12-10**. **No published audit of Folio 5.0.0 was found** — the published list stops at 4.0.0 and Jun 2025. **No published audit of the Reserve Optimistic Governor was found either**: the governance repository carries no `audits/` directory and no releases, and MAG7's governor reports `version() = "1.0.0"`. A standing-partner statement is not a published report. Do not read "the protocol has been audited" as "MAG7's exact deployed code has been audited"; on the evidence available, it has not been shown to be. Reserve's own docs make the general point: "no audit, review, or testing process can eliminate all vulnerabilities or guarantee that the smart contracts are secure, functional, or free from defects."

**What safeguards actually exist on an Index DTF — and one that does not.** Index DTFs have **no pause and no freeze**. The Pauser / Short Freezer / Long Freezer roles and the Paused/Frozen system states belong to Reserve's **Yield** Protocol; the deployed v5 Folio ABI contains no `pause`, `unpause`, `freeze` or `unfreeze` function, and MAG7 has **no mint cap, no supply cap and no redeem cap**. The levers that do exist are:

- **`deprecateFolio()`** — admin-only, and the only kill switch. Because `mint`, `startRebalance`, `openAuction`, `openAuctionUnrestricted`, `bid` and `createTrustedFill` all carry the `notDeprecated` modifier while **`redeem` deliberately does not**, deprecation puts the Folio into **redemption-only mode**: nothing new can be minted and nothing can be rebalanced, but holders can always exit pro rata to the underlying. `isDeprecated()` reads `false` for MAG7 as of 2026-09-16, and the path is exercised in practice — several Base Index DTFs are already marked deprecated.
- **`setBidsEnabled(false)`** — admin-only, a 5.0.0 addition that can switch off permissionless bidding and restrict trading to trusted fillers.
- **`emergencyCloseTrustedFill(address)`** — admin-only, claws back an in-flight async fill.
- **The Guardian's veto** on queued proposals, and its power to revoke an Optimistic Proposer without a vote.

**Upgrades.** MAG7's Folio sits behind a modified transparent proxy whose ERC-1967 admin is a `FolioProxyAdmin`, itself owned by MAG7's governance timelock. Its only external function, `upgradeToVersion(...)`, requires the target implementation to be **registered and not deprecated in Reserve's `FolioVersionRegistry`**. So a MAG7 upgrade needs a successful standard governance proposal, then the timelock delay, and can only land on a version the Reserve-wide registry already blesses. Reserve's docs: "The core contracts are only upgradable via governance proposals that get approved by onchain governance."

**The residual risk the docs name themselves**, and which Section 13 develops: "Because governance powers are broad, governance attacks are possible. For example, an attacker who acquires enough voting power could approve a malicious upgrade that enables theft of funds. Some of this risk is mitigated by special roles within the governance system. That said, these protections only work if the people holding those roles exercise them competently and in good faith."

### 8.9 How to vote-lock RSR and participate in MAG7's governance

Governance of MAG7 is exercised by **RSR vote-lockers**, not by holders of MAG7 as such. Holding MAG7 gives you economic exposure to the basket and **no governance vote**. Vote-locking is entirely optional — holding MAG7 requires none of it — and it is a commitment of a **separate, volatile token**. Nothing here is a solicitation to buy RSR.

**What vote-locking is.** RSR is the default governance token for Reserve Index DTFs, and it is MAG7's. Vote-locking deposits RSR into a **StakingVault**, which issues **vlRSR** ("Vote-Locked Reserve Rights"). Reserve's docs: "Vote-locking commits the chosen token to a specific Index DTF for a minimum period — currently a one-week unlock delay," and "when tokens are locked, the entire balance counts 1-for-1 toward governance power." There is no lock-length multiplier — the entire locked balance counts 1-for-1, and an `amount × time` voting-power model is not how the deployed vault works.

**MAG7's vault, on Base.** The vault is `0x2F0D6538807a77d4AdDCd4b4DAf214Ea2E818E3D` — `name()` "Vote-Locked Reserve Rights", `symbol()` **vlRSR**, whose `asset()` is **RSR on Base, `0xaB36452DbAC151bE02b16Ca17d8919826072f64a`**. Its **unstaking delay is exactly 7 days**, its reward half-life is exactly **7 days**, and as of 2026-09-16 its supply and its assets are identical — **the vlRSR→RSR exchange rate is exactly 1.000000** — against a vault supply of about **33.69 million vlRSR** (33,691,556.890831881720080855, read 2026-09-16). Note that this vault is mapped to more than one Folio entry; MAG7 does not share it with a *different product*, but resolve MAG7 by address rather than by name, symbol or governance vault (Section 13).

**Getting RSR onto Base.** RSR is natively an Ethereum token (`0x320623b8E4fF03373931769A31Fc52A4E78B5d70`). RSR on Base is the **canonical bridged token**: its `l1Token()` is the Ethereum RSR contract and its `bridge()` is Base's standard bridge `0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000010`. Bridge it from **`https://app.reserve.org/bridge`**, which lists **Superbridge** and **Brid.gg** for the Ethereum → Base leg — the same two routes Base's own documentation recommends. Reserve does not operate these bridges; its own page says so: "Bridging involves external providers. Reserve does not control these services and cannot guarantee their security or availability." (Do not use `bridge.base.org`, which Base has deprecated, and do not use the **Portal** card — it targets BNB Smart Chain and is the route for Reserve's BSC DTFs, not for MAG7.) A bridge is not strictly required: RSR trades on Base and the zapper will swap into it, but **Base-side RSR liquidity is thin** — about **$109.1k of reserves across 20 indexed pools**, illustrative and approximate as of 2026-09-16 — so compare the two routes before moving size.

**How to do it, in the Reserve app.**
1. Open MAG7's page at **app.reserve.org**, connect your wallet, and make sure you hold **RSR on Base**.
2. From the **Overview** tab's Governance panel (button: "Lock $RSR to Govern & Earn") or the **Governance** tab's "Govern $MAG7" card, open the vote-lock drawer. It has three tabs — **Vote-lock · Unlock · Delegate**.
3. Enter an RSR amount. You must tick the mandatory checkbox **"I'm aware of the 7-day unlock delay"** before the submit button enables, then **"Approve use of RSR"** and **"Vote lock RSR."** The deposit self-delegates by default, so your voting power is live without a second transaction.
4. Delegation takes **two** addresses — a normal delegate and an **optimistic** delegate — because MAG7 runs the Optimistic Governor with two independent delegation ledgers over the same balance. If you deposit and delegate neither, your tokens vote on neither path.
5.
