## 12. Fees and costs

A complete, costs-inclusive picture. MAG7's *protocol* fees are low, explicit, and readable straight off the contract — but they are only one of **four layers** of cost, and three of the four never appear on a fee panel. Budget for all of them:

1. the **two protocol fees** charged by the Folio itself (Section 12.1);
2. the **platform cut and the split** taken out of those fees before anyone receives them (Section 12.2);
3. the **transaction-level costs** of getting in and out — gas, spread, slippage, dust (Section 12.3);
4. the **issuer's own fee schedule**, one layer beneath the basket, on the Coinbase tokenized stocks MAG7 holds (Section 12.3, and Section 9, *The underlying: Coinbase Tokenized Stocks on Base*).

All onchain values below were read from `0xcef8db49e456f872e288e1c042f916e9ced7c781` on Base on **2026-09-16** and are stated as of that date. Verify any of them yourself — that is the point of putting the raw values in the tables below.

### 12.1 The two protocol fees

**Mint fee — 0.3%.** `mintFee()` returns `3000000000000000`. Folio stores fees on a `1e18` scale, so `3e15 / 1e18 = 0.003` = **0.300% exactly**. It is charged on the shares created when you mint, and it is taken out of those shares rather than added to your bill: a gross mint of 100 MAG7 delivers **99.70 MAG7** to the receiver and 0.30 MAG7 to the fee recipients. It is a one-time cost on the amount minted, and it **does not inflate total supply**. It has fired once and once only — the `MintFeeSet(3e15)` event at deployment, never changed since.

**There is no redemption fee.** The Folio charges exactly two fees — the mint fee and the TVL fee; redeeming MAG7 for its seven underlying tokens costs gas and nothing else at the protocol layer. (The issuer's own 5 bp redemption fee, one layer down, is a different thing and is not open to you — Section 12.3.)

**TVL (management) fee — 0.6% per year.** `tvlFee()` returns `190831821`. That is **not** an annual rate: it is a **per-second** rate on the same `1e18` scale, and the contract converts from the annual rate governance supplies — `tvlFee = 1 − (1 − annualFee)^(1/31 536 000)`, where `ONE_OVER_YEAR = 31 709 791 983 = 1e18 / 31 536 000`. Governance supplied `6000000000000000` = **0.6%/yr**, and the creation transaction logged `TVLFeeSet(190831821, 6000000000000000)`; because the contract stores `floor(190831821.5686…)`, the realized **annual** rate is **0.5999999982%** — 0.6% to twelve significant figures, which is the figure to publish, and the difference is not measurable by a holder.

**How the TVL fee is actually taken — and why "block by block" is wrong for MAG7.** The fee is not withdrawn from the basket. It is **minted as new MAG7 shares**, which dilutes existing holders: `feeShares = supply × 1e18 / (1e18 − tvlFee)^elapsed − supply`. Since Folio 4.0.0 the accrual is **quantized to whole UTC days**, not applied every block — `_accountedUntil = (block.timestamp / 86400) × 86400`, which is why `lastPoke()` reads `1789516800` = **2026-09-16 00:00:00 UTC** exactly. Over a year, the **share count rises by about 0.604%** while **each share's claim on the basket falls by 0.6%**. Those are the same fee seen from two sides. The headline number is **0.6% per year**; the 0.60362% is the dilution rate that produces it, not a second charge.

**Fees accrue whether or not anyone collects them.** As of 2026-09-16 `getPendingFeeShares()` stood at roughly **2.38 MAG7** of accrued, unminted fee shares — one third of that total the DAO's, in `daoPendingFeeShares()`, and two thirds the DTF's recipients', in `feeRecipientsPendingFeeShares()`, the two summing exactly to the whole and the thirds themselves rounding at the wei (illustrative and approximate; the balance grows every day).

`Folio.totalSupply()` **includes** these pending shares; the `totalSupply` field on Reserve's API does not. That is why the two can differ by a couple of MAG7 at any moment and both be correct (see Section 13.1). Distribution is permissionless: the app's **Distribute Fees** panel says, verbatim, *"Distribute accumulated fees to the recipients. Anyone can trigger this transaction."*

**Both fees sit far below the protocol's hard ceilings,** which are compiled into the contract and cannot be exceeded by governance:

| Constant | Value | MAG7 |
|---|---|---|
| `MAX_TVL_FEE` | `0.1e18` = **10%/yr** | 0.6%/yr |
| `MAX_MINT_FEE` | `0.05e18` = **5%** | 0.3% |
| `MIN_MINT_FEE` | `0.0003e18` = **0.03%** | 0.3%, i.e. ten times the floor |
| `MAX_FEE_RECIPIENTS` | `64` | 2 |

One documentation discrepancy, stated plainly because a reader will hit it: Reserve's Index DTF **overview** page gives lower ceilings ("< 5% APY" TVL, "< 1%" mint) than its own FAQ, its platform-fee page, and the deployed constants above. **The deployed contract is authoritative** at 10%/yr and 5%. Where Reserve's official documentation and this document disagree, the official sources control — so if that page is corrected, follow it.

### 12.2 The platform fee, the split, and the RSR buy-and-burn

**The platform fee is taken *out of* the two fees above, not added on top of them.** Reserve's protocol documentation is explicit: *"A platform fee is applied to both the TVL and Mint fees before they are distributed to recipients."* Only the remainder reaches the DTF's own governance-chosen recipients.

MAG7 reads the platform fee live from the Index DTF fee registry at `0x0262E3e15cCFD2221b35D05909222f1f5FCdcd80`:

| `getFeeDetails(0xCEF8Db49…)` | Raw | Decoded |
|---|---|---|
| `feeNumerator` / `feeDenominator` | `333333333333333333` / `1000000000000000000` | **33.3333333333333333%** of both fee streams |
| `feeFloor` | `1000000000000000` | **0.1% annual** lower bound on the platform's take |
| `recipient` | `0xcBCa96091f43C024730a020E57515A18b5dC633B` | the protocol fee recipient |

`getFeeDetails(address(0))` — the registry default — returns the identical tuple, so **MAG7 is on the protocol default with no per-DTF override**. (A note for anyone reading Reserve's own codebase: `FALLBACK_PLATFORM_FEES[Base] = 50` appears in the app's constants, but it is a fallback used only if the onchain read fails. The live registry value is 33.33%.)

**The remainder goes to exactly two recipients.** `feeRecipients(0)` and `feeRecipients(1)` return the pair below; `feeRecipients(2)` reverts, which is how you know the list is complete:

| Index | Address | Portion (D18) | Share of the remainder |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | `0x0E0c281ff05d34729CD764dcFC4Fa999b720407d` | `300000000000000100` | **30%** |
| 1 | `0xEAfA84184BEb90891cb5c4942ebD59C18dda0bfE` | `699999999999999900` | **70%** |

**Read the next point carefully, because the chain and the app quote the same split in two different bases.** The chain's 30/70 is a share of what is left *after* the platform's 33.33%; the app's **Fees & Revenue Distribution** panel restates every portion as a share of the *gross* fee, and so shows different-looking numbers that are arithmetically identical. On the app's base the platform is taken first as the **Fixed Platform Share 33.33%**, `0xEAfA8418…` becomes 70% × 66.667% = **Governance Share 46.67%**, `0x0E0c281f…` becomes 30% × 66.667% = **Other recipient 1 20%**, and the **Deployer Share is 0%**. Both are right. Neither is a different fee. The app's four figures sum to 100% (33.33 + 46.67 + 0 + 20), and Reserve's own settings code produces them by dividing each onchain portion by `100 / (100 − platformFee)`.

**Where every fee dollar actually lands,** expressed as percentages of what you pay:

| Stream | Gross | Platform / DAO → RSR buy-and-burn | `0xEAfA8418…` (governance) | `0x0E0c281f…` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Mint fee** | 0.30% per mint | 0.10% | 0.14% | 0.06% |
| **TVL fee** | 0.60% per year | 0.20%/yr | 0.28%/yr | 0.12%/yr |

**One subtlety that is easy to state backwards.** On the mint path the platform's claim is `max(its 1/3 share, the fee floor)`. With a 0.30% mint fee those two are *the same number*: one-third of 0.30% is 0.10%, and the floor is 0.10%. **The floor binds exactly; it does not raise the platform's cut above its one-third share.** On the TVL path the floor is annualized before the comparison and 0.6% is well above 0.1%, so the floor does not bind at all. (A separate discrepancy worth knowing: Reserve's docs and the Folio 5.0.0 header describe a 0.15% / 15 bps floor, but `defaultFeeFloor()` reads `1e15` = **0.10%** live on Base, Ethereum and BNB Smart Chain. Trust the chain.)

**What the platform's share does.** Reserve's documentation describes it without ambiguity: platform fees on Index DTFs are *"swept into a protocol-level contract that automatically uses them to market-buy RSR and then sends the purchased tokens to the burn address, permanently removing them from circulation,"* and *"because the burn mechanism lives at the protocol layer, it applies to every Index DTF — regardless of which governance token the fund chooses."* So a third of every fee MAG7 charges funds an RSR buy-and-burn. The percentage that feeds the burn is itself governed onchain.

**What the governance share does.** `0xEAfA8418…` is the **TokenJar of the Base vlRSR staking vault** — verified two ways: the vault at `0x2F0D6538807a77d4AdDCd4b4DAf214Ea2E818E3D` returns it from `tokenJar()`, and that jar's `token()` is RSR on Base (`0xaB36452DbAC151bE02b16Ca17d8919826072f64a`). Fees routed there are intended to be swapped into RSR through the trusted-filler registry — a permissionless call that anyone can make — after which the RSR streams into the vault as a native reward and raises the **vlRSR/RSR exchange rate**. There is no claim button and no reward token to harvest (`getAllRewardTokens()` returns an empty array; RSR cannot be registered as a reward token because it is the vault's own `asset()`). As of 2026-09-16 no conversion has been run: the jar holds **10.263699410782686498 MAG7 and 0 RSR**, and the vault's exchange rate is still exactly **1.000000**. Distribution to governance holders follows an *"exponential distribution model over time to mitigate MEV attacks."* See Section 8, *What a DTF is (the Reserve Index Protocol), end to end*.

**The third recipient is unlabeled, and this document does not label it.** `0x0E0c281ff05d34729CD764dcFC4Fa999b720407d` receives 30% of the post-platform remainder — **20% of gross fees**. The Reserve app renders it as *"Other recipient 1"* with no name attached, because it is neither the DTF's deployer nor the staking vault's jar. What is onchain and checkable is its structure: it is a **Gnosis Safe v1.4.1 with a threshold of 1 and a single owner**, `0xb3CF59A5f12cA319861376C5e63Eef4790a42B44` — the same Safe that holds MAG7's Auction Launcher, Brand Manager and Timelock Canceller roles and sits among its optimistic proposers. Who ultimately controls that Safe carries no public label anywhere, and this document names no one. It is disclosed again, as a conflict, in Section 15, *Conflicts of interest and disclosures*.

**The split is governable and has already changed.** Three `FeeRecipientsSet` events exist: at deployment (2026-08-04), at the governance migration just under fifty minutes later, and on **2026-08-17**, when an executed proposal moved the portions from `298507462686567140 / 701492537313432860` to the round `300000000000000100 / 699999999999999900` in force today. Fee recipients are set **per DTF**, by that DTF's own governance, and nothing about MAG7's arrangement carries over to any other one. **MAG7's three-way split is specific to MAG7** — a platform share, a governance share and a third, unlabeled recipient — and a reader who wants another DTF's split should read it off that DTF's own **Fees & Revenue Distribution** panel rather than assuming this one. Verify MAG7's own live figures in MAG7's panel at app.reserve.org before relying on any of them.

### 12.3 Transaction-level costs (easy to overlook)

- **Onchain gas.** Every mint, redeem, buy, or sell is a Base transaction paid in **ETH**, not USDC — you need a small ETH balance on Base even if you pay for MAG7 in stablecoins. Base gas is cheap: KyberSwap's own gas estimates for single-token swaps on Base on 2026-09-16 ran **$0.005–$0.034 per swap** (illustrative and approximate; gas varies with congestion). A MAG7 mint is a much larger transaction than a single swap because it sources seven underlying tokens — the Reserve zapper estimated roughly **3.0 million gas units** for a 100 USDC buy.
- **Zapper spread and slippage.** The Reserve zapper converts whatever token you pay with into the seven underlyings. On 2026-09-16, a 100 USDC quote returned **0.9644166 MAG7** worth **$99.82** with a **true price impact of −0.12%** and **zero dust**. Two structural points matter more than the numbers: **buying MAG7 through the app mints new shares rather than trading the pool** (Section 8.4), and the zapper's `slippage` parameter uses an **inverse-fraction convention** — `200` means 0.5%, not 2%.
- **The secondary market is the expensive way in, and the constrained way out.** MAG7's only live venue is one Aerodrome Slipstream pool holding roughly **$420k** with about **$5k of 24-hour volume** (2026-09-16). Measured KyberSwap aggregator quotes that day: a $1,000 buy filled near **103.87/MAG7** and a $10,000 buy near **104.19**, against a reported NAV of **103.45** — roughly **+0.4%** and **+0.7%** over NAV, with a round-trip spread near **0.6%** on a $5,000 sell. **For anything above a few thousand dollars, minting is cheaper than buying the pool.** Selling is the constrained side, and the honest thing is to **own the bound rather than the warning: the largest sell measured for this document was $5,000, and no aggregator quote above that size was taken.** Do not extrapolate the 0.6% round-trip upward — in a pool holding roughly $420k, price impact is not linear in size, and a reader sizing an exit materially larger than that has no measured figure here and should read the live quote before signing. The redemption route of Section 10.4 is the exit that does not consume this pool at all. All figures illustrative and approximate as of 2026-09-16; see Section 10, *How to buy, sell, and redeem MAG7*.
- **Dust.** Reserve's documentation notes it is common to receive dust amounts on the order of **1–10 bps** of input value from routing. The quotes above returned none, so treat it as possible rather than certain.
- **Rebalance execution is a cost, but not a fee.** MAG7's rebalances run as onchain auctions against the basket itself. Nobody bills you for them; they show up as **tracking error** against a perfectly equal-weight basket, bounded by the price bands the rebalance manager sets and the 15-minute auction length (`auctionLength() = 900`). MAG7 also holds a meaningful fraction of some very small tokenized-stock floats — about **5.3% of all TSLAc in existence** on 2026-09-16 — which means its own rebalances can move the pools it trades in. See Section 7, *Methodology: equal weight, seven names, two months*.
- **The issuer's fee schedule, one layer down.** The seven tokens MAG7 holds are securities issued under a prospectus, and that prospectus carries its own **"Expenses charged to Holders"** table. These are **not** charged by Reserve, do not appear in MAG7's 0.3% / 0.6%, and are not billed to you on any transaction you send — but they are the economics of the assets in the basket:

| Issuer-layer item | Rate | Who bears it |
|---|---|---|
| Creation fee | **0.01% (1 bp)** of the aggregate market price of Underlying delivered | Authorised Participants creating tokens at the primary layer |
| Redemption fee | **0.05% (5 bp)** | Vested Holders redeeming at the primary layer |
| Distribution fee | **5.0% of the gross aggregate value** of dividends, charged *"before any withholding taxes, deductions, or reinvestment"* | every token holder, including MAG7's basket |
| US withholding tax on dividends | **30%** for non-US Holders unless reduced by treaty | same |
| UAE withholding tax | **none at the current time** | — |
| Ongoing management fee | **not listed in the published table** | — |

  Four things to hold onto. **Dividends are never paid out** — they are reinvested and reflected as an increase in the Deposit Ratio, so the 5% distribution fee plus withholding is a drag on that reinvestment, not a missing cash payment (this document quotes no dividend yield; see Section 12.4). **"No ongoing fee" is the right reading of the published table, not a guarantee** — the prospectus's own NAV-divergence risk factor lists *"management fees, transaction costs, taxes, and other expenses"* among the reasons the Deposit Ratio may fail to track. **None of these rates is contractually fixed:** *"The Issuer reserves the right to modify, reduce, or increase its fees upon thirty (30) days' notice to Holders."* And **creation and redemption are not open to you** — a secondary buyer holds as an Unvested Holder with no right to redeem for shares, which is covered in full in Section 9 and Section 13.

### 12.4 No performance or yield figures

Consistent with how Reserve markets these products, **this document quotes no performance, return, or yield figures for MAG7 anywhere** — not since-inception or monthly returns, not NAV highs or lows, not a staking APR, not a liquidity-pool APY, not the underlying companies' historical returns. There are none stated here because past or projected returns are not represented and would not be reliable, and because MAG7 has existed since **2026-08-04** — far too short a history for any return figure to mean anything.

Three specific traps, named because they look sanctioned:

- **Figures rendered inside Reserve's own app are still performance figures.** The Overview page's price-change cells, the factsheet's monthly table, and the Governance tab's vote-lock APR are all excluded here, and so is the app's APR button text.
- **The pre-inception NAV series is synthetic.** Reserve's own code describes it as backfilled, and market cap and supply read zero for every date before 2026-08-04. No chart drawn from it is described in this document.
- **The TVL fee is a cost, not a payout.** MAG7 is not a yield product. Vote-locked RSR governors are the intended recipients of part of it; **MAG7 holders pay it.** As of 2026-09-16 none of it has yet been converted or accrued to them (Section 12.2).

Any third-party figure cited elsewhere in this document — company financials, market caps, industry size — is about the *underlying businesses*, not about MAG7's return, and is attributed inline with its source and its as-of date.

### 12.5 Putting the fees in context

The 0.3% mint and 0.6%/yr TVL fee are a real, ongoing cost, and the honest way to judge them is against every other way to hold these seven names. On **$10,000, in year one**, as of 2026-09-16 — all figures illustrative and approximate, and derived from measured quotes and published fee schedules rather than from any model:

| Route | Up-front | Ongoing | ≈ Year one | Available to |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **The seven Coinbase tokenized stocks, bought individually on Base** | ~0.1% execution (~$10) + a few cents of gas | **none published** | **≈ $10** | a Base wallet, inside the issuer's Reg S perimeter (Section 11, *Eligibility: who can and cannot buy*) |
| **MAG7, minted through the app** | 0.3% (**$30**) + gas | 0.6%/yr (**$60**) | **≈ $90** | the same perimeter, plus Reserve's own per-DTF geo-block at its app |
| **MAG7, bought on the Aerodrome pool** | ~0.7% over NAV (**~$70**) at this size | 0.6%/yr (**$60**) | **≈ $130** | the same perimeter |
| **MAGS (Roundhill Magnificent Seven ETF)** | $0 commission at most brokers | **0.30%/yr** total operating expenses | **≈ $30** | anyone with a brokerage account for US-listed securities, **including US persons** |
| **Invesco QQQ, a broad proxy** (~38% Mag-7 weight, reported, etf.com) | $0 commission at most brokers | **0.18%/yr** total expense ratio (Invesco's own page) | **≈ $18** | same |

**Read the table the unflattering way first.** For a reader who has every option, **MAG7 is the most expensive route on this list** — roughly nine times the cost of assembling the seven tokenized stocks yourself, and about three times the cost of a US-listed ETF that tracks the same seven names. MAG7's case does not rest on price. It rests on the reader **not** having every option.

**What the fee actually buys.** Assembling the basket yourself is cheap to execute and free to hold, but **keeping it equal-weight is the work**: seven legs per reset, each with a price check, a weight calculation, a route, an approval and a signature — and MAG7's mandate contemplates six resets a year, not four. The direct trading cost of that is small; the cost is attention and discipline, and the discipline matters most precisely when it is hardest to maintain. MAG7 bundles the buying, the weighting and the rebalancing into **one position, one approval and one tax lot**. Because rebalancing happens at the folio level and never touches your balance, a DIY rebalancer realizes a disposal on every sell leg while a MAG7 holder does not — a genuine structural difference, though **whether it matters to you is entirely a question of your own jurisdiction's tax law, and nothing here is tax advice.**

**What the fee does not buy.** It does not remove the issuer, custody and redemption-right problems described in Section 9 and Section 13 — those are identical whether you hold MAG7 or the seven tokens directly. It does not buy liquidity: on 2026-09-16 the MAG7 pool traded about **$5k** in 24 hours while NVDAc alone traded about **$16.2m** (reported, GeckoTerminal, 2026-09-16), so **the wrapper is far less liquid than its own ingredients.** And it does not buy the protections a brokerage gives you — no investor-compensation scheme, no tax forms, no shareholder rights.

**The comparison to MAGS cuts both ways, and both directions are worth stating.** MAGS charges 0.30% a year against MAG7's 0.6%, and reported AUM of about **$4.34 billion** against MAG7's market cap of roughly **$390k** (AUM reported by stockanalysis.com; both as of 2026-09-16) — it is cheaper and vastly deeper. But MAGS is an **actively managed** fund that rebalances to equal weight **quarterly** and, by its own prospectus, gains its exposure primarily through **swap agreements and/or forward contracts** rather than by holding seven stocks, and may invest up to 100% of its assets in US Treasuries, money-market funds and short-term bond ETFs; its reported holdings on 2026-09-16 show roughly **47% in Treasury bills** (stockanalysis.com). It discloses counterparty concentration and warns that cash creations may raise its capital-gain distributions. And the eligibility is inverted: **US persons can buy MAGS and cannot buy MAG7**, while EU and UK retail investors generally cannot buy a US-listed ETF at all for want of a PRIIPs Key Information Document (reported). Neither product is strictly better. They serve different people, and the fee is only one column of the comparison — see Section 2.1 for the full head-to-head.

---

## 13. Risks (read this in full)

MAG7 stacks five distinct risk surfaces on top of one another, and a reader who understands only one of them will misjudge the product. The **theme** is seven mega-cap US companies exposed to a single capital-expenditure cycle (Section 13.2, Section 13.3). The **wrapper** is a Reserve Index DTF — a smart contract governed by vote-locked RSR (Section 13.4, Section 13.6, Section 13.8, Section 13.15, Section 13.17). The **underlying** is not shares but seven Coinbase tokenized stocks, securities issued under an ADGM-approved prospectus by an issuer that owes a secondary holder considerably less than an ETF owes its shareholder (Section 13.5, Section 13.12, Section 13.16). The **market** is one Aerodrome pool on Base, reached through the ordinary **onchain environment** — MEV, irreversible transactions, and a front-end that can be spoofed (Section 13.9, Section 13.10, Section 13.11). And cutting across all four is **pricing** — a 24/5 oracle behind a 24/7 token, and a per-token pause flag in a registry that neither Reserve nor you control (Section 13.7, Section 13.13, Section 13.14).

The list below is substantial but **not exhaustive.** Reserve's own risk documentation at **https://docs.reserve.org/risks** and its terms at **https://reserve.org/terms_and_conditions** are the controlling sources for the platform, and the seven **ADGM-approved prospectuses** (linked in Section 18) are the controlling sources for the underlying. You should read them. **If you do not fully understand a risk, do not use the product.**

### 13.1 Total-loss and volatility risk

MAG7 is an **experimental, concentrated, seven-name basket of tokenized securities.** It can be **highly volatile, illiquid, and may lose value entirely.** Do not invest money you cannot afford to lose. It is **not a deposit**, it is **not an ETF and not regulated as one**, and it is **not insured by the FDIC, SIPC, or anyone else.**

The scale is part of the risk. Illustrative and approximate **as of 2026-09-16**: a market capitalization of about **$390k**, a `Folio.totalSupply()` of roughly **3,783 MAG7** — a figure that includes accrued-but-unminted fee shares and therefore sits slightly above the minted-only supply Reserve's API and CoinGecko report — and **99 addresses holding a non-zero balance** at block 51,393,279. This is a very small product, and small products have thin markets, few holders, and less of the operational redundancy that scale eventually buys — see Section 13.11.

The SIPC point needs a specific correction, because the presence of a US broker-dealer in the stack invites the wrong inference. The shares behind the underlying tokens are custodied at **Alpaca Securities LLC**, an SEC-registered broker-dealer and a FINRA and SIPC member. **The prospectus is explicit that there is no pass-through SIPC benefit to Holders.** SIPC membership protects Alpaca's own brokerage customers; a MAG7 holder is not one.

### 13.2 Concentration and correlation risk — equal weight is not diversification

MAG7 holds **seven names**, each with a target weight of **1/7 ≈ 14.29%**. Seven is a small number, and the seven are not seven independent bets. They are seven of the largest companies in **one country**, reporting in **one currency**, under **one regulatory regime**, and — as Section 4 sets out from their own filings — largely exposed to **one AI capital-expenditure cycle**, with a circular ring of customer, supplier and investor relationships running between them.

**Equal weighting flattens the dollar split; it does not touch the shared factor.** Section 7.3 measures what that is worth, and the answer is about one extra effective name out of seven — a large change per name and a small one in aggregate, because all seven capitalizations sit inside one order of magnitude. Seven equal slices of one factor is still one factor. That is why "equal weight is not diversification here" is one of the three caveats this document asks any reader, human or machine, to carry with every claim.

Equal weighting also makes an **active** bet that a cap-weighted holder does not. At a 14.29% target, MAG7 carries roughly **6.9× the Tesla exposure** an S&P 500 index fund does (against ~2.1% of the index, third-party data, as of 2026-09-16) and about **35% less NVIDIA** than a cap-weighted version of the same seven. Whatever is true of Tesla — the highest-multiple, lowest-margin, most China-exposed name in the basket, per Section 6 — is amplified here.

On correlation, one caution matters more than any statistic. **Daily correlation is not correlation in a drawdown.** Mega-cap correlations have historically *risen* in sell-offs, precisely when diversification is supposed to help; a period of low measured co-movement is not a promise that the basket will spread risk in the next shock. Section 3.4 and Section 4 handle the measured dispersion evidence and state their method; neither is a forecast. Michael Hartnett, who coined the "Magnificent Seven" label, put the crowding version of this risk most plainly in his 2024 Reuters interview: *"You can only sell what you own, and everyone owns the seven."* **Reported, one strategist's view, and not a prediction about MAG7.**

### 13.3 Theme and market risk: the AI capital-expenditure cycle

The theme could be wrong, or already priced in, or right on a timeline longer than a holder's patience. Section 3.5 states the bear case in full; the short version is that **the spending is verified and the monetization is not.** Four of the seven are funding the same build-out at a scale visible in their own filings; one sells into it; and the published estimates of what the AI-native companies buying that capacity actually earn are far smaller than the capital being committed. Those revenue-versus-capex aggregates are **third-party estimates over differently-defined measures and are attributed as such wherever this document uses them** — they are statements about industry size, never about MAG7's price.

Three further theme risks are specific enough to name. **Valuations are elevated** on the multiples reported by third-party data providers, and one name in the basket trades at a forward multiple several times the next-highest — with the same 14.29% target as the cheapest. **Depreciation schedules are an accounting judgment** on hardware with a much shorter product cycle, and small changes to them move reported earnings at several of the largest spenders at once; Section 3.5 gives the filings on both sides of that argument, which do not all point the same way. And **antitrust, China exposure and legal overhang differ enormously between the seven**, while the weighting rule is entirely indifferent to which name carries which.

**Market-size growth, even if it happens, does not translate into token returns.** This document contains no forecast of MAG7's value and no performance figure of any kind (Section 12.4).

### 13.4 Tracking and NAV-deviation risk

MAG7's market price can **trade above or below** the value of its underlying basket, and its live weights routinely differ from its target weights. Both are structural.

- **Weights drift between rebalances, by design.** Each rebalance sets **fixed quantities** of each token per MAG7 share — `rebalanceControl().weightControl` reads **false**, which makes MAG7 a *tracking* DTF whose quantities are fixed at proposal time. Prices then move and the percentages drift. On **2026-09-16** the live basket spanned roughly **13.4% to 15.6%** against the 14.29% target — a spread of about **2.1 percentage points** — read from Reserve's API and stamped, because the exact number moves with every price tick.
- **Even at reset, the basket is not exactly one-seventh.** Valuing the most recent rebalance's encoded targets at that rebalance's own mid prices gives legs of **14.15% to 14.76%**, a **0.61 pp** spread. The honest description of MAG7's weighting is *"one-seventh by value at the price snapshot used when the rebalance was proposed"* — not "exactly 1/7".
- **A rebalance can be proposed and simply not happen.** Of the three opened to date, the middle one was granted a 24-hour window, ran **zero auctions**, and expired having moved nothing. Nothing onchain schedules, times, or guarantees a rebalance; the two-month figure is a sentence in the contract's `mandate()` string, not a mechanism (Section 7.4).
- **Arbitrage narrows these gaps but does not close them.** Mint and redeem are permissionless and atomic at the DTF, so a large enough dislocation is profitable to correct. In a market this thin, "large enough" can be a long way from zero — and off-hours, when the mark behind the basket stops moving altogether (Section 13.14), the onchain price floats free of it.

**There is no guarantee that MAG7 tracks its intended basket**, and no mechanism anywhere in the product that promises it will.

### 13.5 Issuer, custodian and redemption-right risk (Coinbase tokenized stocks)

This is the risk with no ETF analog, and it is the single most important structural fact in this document.

The seven tokens in MAG7's basket are **securities**, issued by **Coinbase Onchain SPV Ltd** (ADGM company 37941) under a prospectus approved by the **ADGM Financial Services Regulatory Authority**, with **Onchain Marketplace Limited** as Tokenisation Entity and CSD and **Alpaca Securities LLC** as broker and custodian. The backing is real: shares held at a regulated US broker-custodian, in what Coinbase describes as *"a bankruptcy-remote structure, in a trust for token holders."*

**The backing is not your claim.** Securities are offered *"exclusively to Authorised Participants"*, redemption belongs to **Vested Holders** only, and **a person who buys MAG7 on the secondary market is an Unvested Holder of the underlying with no path to the shares.** Vested status, where it exists at all, is conditional and revocable — the conditions may change even after a Holder has satisfied them, *"which may result in the freezing or confiscation of the Securities."* Section 9.3, *Redemption and the value anchor — Vested and Unvested Holders*, quotes the prospectus in full. Beyond the rights question, ordinary issuer and custodian risk applies: you have **no contractual relationship with the issuer at all**, your exposure runs through a smart contract that holds its tokens, and Reserve's own risk string names the exposure — *"Custodial risks - Tokens are claims on underlying shares held by a regulated custodian, whose failure or insolvency could delay settlement or cause loss of value."*

### 13.6 Smart-contract and protocol risk

DTFs run on smart contracts, which may contain coding errors, design defects, or vulnerabilities that could cause exploits, malfunctions, or **total or partial loss of assets**, despite audits. Reserve's own documentation makes the point without hedging: *"no audit, review, or testing process can eliminate all vulnerabilities or guarantee that the smart contracts are secure, functional, or free from defects."*

**The audit position, in one line.** The Index protocol has five published third-party reviews, the last covering Folio **4.0.0**; **MAG7 runs Folio 5.0.0, released 2025-12-10, for which no published audit was found**, and the Reserve Optimistic Governor repository carries no `audits/` directory at all. Section 8.8, *Security and audits*, sets out what is and is not published, and why a standing-partner statement is not a report. Do not read "the Reserve protocol has been audited" as "MAG7's deployed code has been audited."

**One correction matters for anyone reasoning by analogy with Reserve's other products: Index DTFs have no pause and no freeze.** The Pauser, Short Freezer and Long Freezer roles belong to Reserve's *Yield* Protocol; the only emergency lever here is **`deprecateFolio()`**, which puts the Folio into **redemption-only mode** — nothing can be minted or rebalanced, but `redeem` is deliberately not gated, so holders can always exit pro rata to the underlying tokens (Section 8.8). Against that sits the protocol's own guidance: *"the actual behavior may vary from the intended behavior, and it's safest to wait for an application to be in use for a long period of time before trusting it to behave as expected."* MAG7's contract was deployed on **2026-08-04**.

**Third-party dependencies** compound all of this: Base itself, the **B20** native-precompile token standard the seven c-tokens use, Chainlink's feeds, the Aerodrome pool, the zapper's routing, and the trusted-filler mechanism used in rebalance auctions. Any of them could fail, be exploited, or become unavailable. The trusted-filler design carries its own published caveat: fills happen out-of-band and the contracts *"enforce protocol restrictions and limitations on them when they perform the swap via EIP-1271. However, adherence to EIP-1271 by the filler cannot be enforced."* MAG7 has `trustedFillerEnabled() = true`; **which filler implementations are registered cannot be enumerated from the registry**, so this document describes the mechanism and names no counterparty.

### 13.7 Oracle, MEV, slippage, and execution risk

DTFs and their rebalances rely on pricing inputs and onchain execution. **Faulty, stale, or manipulated price data could cause incorrect behavior.** Reserve reports MAG7's per-asset price sourcing as `chainlink, zapper, alchemy, defillama`; the tokenized stocks themselves are marked by Chainlink's Coinbase equity feeds (Section 13.13, Section 13.14). Reserve's own risk string for this layer, verbatim: *"Valuation & NAV risks - Pricing relies on oracles (Chainlink feeds) that can be stale or paused during corporate actions, distorting marks and mint/redeem pricing."*

Rebalances execute as **15-minute onchain Dutch auctions** inside price bands set when the rebalance is proposed — on the most recent rebalance, a 16× range against the protocol's 100× ceiling (Section 7.5). The protocol's own documentation explains why the band is a risk and not just a parameter: *"If the price of an asset rises above its `high` price, this can result in a loss of value for Folio holders due to the auction price curve on a token pair starting at too low a price."* A band set too wide, or a market that moves outside it, costs holders value at execution.

For you as a transactor: transactions on Base can be subject to **MEV** (front-running, sandwich attacks), **slippage**, **failed or partial execution**, and value extraction. **You are responsible for your transaction settings, including slippage tolerances.** In a market with one thin pool, the quote is the thing to read — see Section 13.11 and Section 10.7.

### 13.8 Governance risk

DTF governance powers are broad, so **governance attacks are possible.** Reserve's own risk documentation states it directly: *"Because governance powers are broad, governance attacks are possible. For example, an attacker who acquires enough voting power could approve a malicious upgrade that enables theft of funds. Some of this risk is mitigated by special roles within the governance system. That said, these protections only work if the people holding those roles exercise them competently and in good faith. Before using a DTF, users should review who holds these roles on the Details + Roles page and the Governance page in the App."*

That is the whole of the generic risk, and it applies to MAG7 as it applies to any DTF. The safeguards that mitigate it — a Guardian that can cancel queued proposals and revoke a compromised proposer, a timelock, a per-proposer throttle, hard protocol ceilings, and an allowlist that only slow governance can extend — carry exactly the condition the quotation names. Governance decisions also shape what you own rather than merely how the product is administered, which is treated separately in Section 13.15. **Section 8.10 lists MAG7's governance configuration as deployed, and Section 15 discloses the conflicts of interest that sit around it. Read both, and review the live role-holders on the app before relying on any of it.**

### 13.9 Frontend, interface and user-error risk

The **app.reserve.org** front-end is built and maintained by **Reserve (ABC Labs, LLC)** — not an unrelated third party — and relies on third-party services under the hood. Even so, any web front-end can contain bugs or be spoofed, compromised, censored, or unavailable, potentially inducing you to sign incorrect transactions or approve unintended permissions. Onchain transactions are generally **irreversible**: user error — wrong address, wrong network, a bad approval — can cause permanent loss. Use a self-custody wallet, and read what you are signing.

**MAG7 carries an address-matching hazard that is worse than the usual imposter-token warning, because the lookalikes are not imposters at all.** As of 2026-09-16:

| Address on Base | `name()` / `symbol()` | Supply | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| **`0xCEF8Db49E456f872E288E1C042F916E9ceD7c781`** | Reserve Magnificent 7 DTF (Base) / MAG7 | ≈ 3,783 | ✅ **This is MAG7** — the only one in Reserve's API, the app or CoinGecko |
| `0x4ca35589de6d2c2cb4d3063ab279590b627652bc` | Reserve Magnificent 7 DTF (Base) / MAG7 | **0** | Not the product. A live, **un-deprecated** Folio with a USDC-only basket at zero balance and its own separate governance admin |
| `0x9adfced43129d7d07a565e93f8d731959aef5a66` | Reserve Magnificent 7 DTF (Base) / MAG7 | **0** | Not the product. Same shape as the row above |
| `0x9e6a46f294bb67c20f1d1e7afb0bbef614403b55` | MAG7.ssi / MAG7.ssi | not stated | **A different token from a different issuer**, unrelated to Reserve, and as of 2026-09-16 carrying materially more onchain liquidity than MAG7's own pool — so a ticker search lands on the deeper, wrong market. This document has not verified who issues it and characterizes it no further |

**Three live contracts on Base carry the identical name, the identical symbol, the identical `mandate()` string and the identical fee parameters.** All three were deployed on 2026-08-04 by the same address through the same deployer; none is marked deprecated onchain; only one has any supply. Why the other two exist is not publicly established and this document does not speculate. The operational consequence is the point: **anything that resolves "MAG7 on Base" by name, by symbol, or by governance vault rather than by address can land on the wrong contract.** The same discipline applies one layer down, to the seven underlying tokens — Base's own registry page puts it plainly: *"The full list of Coinbase Tokenized Stocks on Base. Match the contract address before you buy. If a token is not on this list, Coinbase did not issue it."*

**Verify `0xcef8db49e456f872e288e1c042f916e9ced7c781` before you approve anything. Do not search by ticker.**

### 13.10 Regulatory and eligibility risk

Tokenized equities and DTFs are novel and operate in an **evolving regulatory environment.** Rules can change, products can become restricted or unavailable in additional jurisdictions, and **MAG7 is already prohibited for US persons** (Section 11). Reserve's own risk string for this layer, verbatim: *"Regulatory & compliance risks - Tokenized equities face evolving rules and are restricted to eligible non-US users, which can force restricted access, halted redemptions, or unwinding."*

Four specific features of MAG7's eligibility regime are risks in their own right:

- **The perimeter is set by the issuer, not by Reserve**, and the underlying tokens are offered under **Regulation S** — not for U.S. Persons, not inside the U.S., not for the account or benefit of any U.S. Person. Every acquirer is *deemed to represent* all three.
- **No per-country eligibility table is published by anyone** — not Coinbase, not Base, not Reserve. The prospectus uses the phrase *"prohibited jurisdictions"* without ever defining or enumerating it. The public floors that do exist are the six named sanctions authorities, comprehensively sanctioned or embargoed countries, and the **FATF "call for action"** list — **DPRK, Iran and Myanmar** as of the FATF plenary statement of **19 June 2026**, restated at each plenary and therefore subject to change.
- **The issuer can narrow the perimeter at any time.** Prospectus §14.1(d), verbatim: *"The Issuer reserves the right to impose additional restrictions on the acquisition, holding, transfer, or resale of the Securities at any time… Any such additional restrictions may include restrictions relating to particular jurisdictions, persons, Wallet addresses, custodians, intermediaries, trading venues, or settlement systems."* Those restrictions would be published at coinbase.com/tokenize and through the ADGM Listing Authority's disclosure platform — **which is access-restricted to issuers and reporting entities**, though ADGM separately maintains a public disclosures register whose contents this document has not been able to search (Section 11). You may not see a change before it binds you.
- **Enforcement is real and it is server-side.** Reserve's compliance API applies a **per-DTF** geolocation gate: as of 2026-09-16 a US IP receives `{"restricted":true,"restriction":"geolocation-prohibited"}` for MAG7 while the platform-wide check returns `restricted:false`. At the token layer, B20 transfer policies can block specific addresses and a blocked transfer reverts. **The in-app check for your own wallet and location is the only authoritative answer**, and it can change without notice.

**You are responsible for your own legal and tax compliance**, including the tax treatment of a token that never distributes cash (Section 13.16). Nothing in this document is legal or tax advice.

### 13.11 Liquidity, depth, and concentration of supply

MAG7 has **exactly one live trading venue** — a single Aerodrome Slipstream MAG7/USDC pool holding roughly **$420k** against about **$5k** of 24-hour volume as of 2026-09-16, with **no centralized-exchange listing of any kind** — and an extraordinarily concentrated holder base: **two addresses held about 97.6% of supply** at block 51,393,279, one of them the pool itself and one an unattributed **2-of-3 multisig** for which this document names no operator and offers no theory. The number is the risk: a supply this concentrated means a single decision by a single holder can dominate the market for the token. Note also that pool TVL counts the USDC side and is therefore *larger* than the DTF's own market capitalization — never read "$420k of liquidity" as assets under management. Buying mints new shares and does not consume pool depth; **selling is the constrained side, so check live depth and the quoted price impact before sizing an exit**, and remember that redeeming to the seven underlying tokens is the exit that depends on no pool at all (Section 10.7, Example D) — though it leaves you holding tokenized stocks with their own, separate liquidity. The pool's AERO gauge emissions are paid in AERO by Aerodrome and not by Reserve, and providing liquidity is a different activity with different risks from holding MAG7. Section 10.9, *Liquidity and market depth*, has the full figures and the disclaimers around them.

Reserve's own risk string for this layer, verbatim: *"Liquidity risks - On-chain trading is 24/7, but mint/redeem may be limited or windowed, and in stressed markets token prices can deviate from the underlying equities."*

### 13.12 Transfer-control risk at the token layer: freeze, seize, cancel, redesignate

The seven underlying tokens are not permissionless assets in the way an ordinary ERC-20 is. The Tokenisation Entity holds blacklist, freeze, pause and seize powers that operate **without requiring counterparty cooperation**, may rescind a transfer, cancel Securities or redesignate a Vested Holder **with or without payment**, and may deny a prohibited holder all economic benefit *"until the position is no longer prohibited (which may never occur)."* Every one of those quotations, and the graduated intervention protocol behind them, is set out in full in Section 9.5, *Transfer restrictions, policy gating, freeze and seize*.

**Why it reaches you.** These powers are exercisable against the wallets that hold the c-tokens, and the wallet holding MAG7's c-tokens is the Folio contract itself. An intervention against basket assets would impair the basket, and no holder of MAG7 is a party to any of it. Today the controls are reactive rather than preventive — secondary holding and trading are permissionless, with KYC only at the Authorized Participant mint and redeem flows — but that is the current configuration, not a guarantee.

### 13.13 Oracle-pause risk on the pricing path

The price of each underlying token is defined as **the underlying equity's market price multiplied by a per-token multiplier**, and that multiplier is read from Coinbase's own onchain oracle registry on Base — a registry that also exposes a **per-token pause flag** behind a `PAUSER_ROLE` held by a **single address**. **This document does not attribute that address to any named entity, because no public source identifies it.**

**A pause on the pricing path is a live, unilateral switch held outside both Reserve and the holder**: no MAG7 governance vote, and no function on the Folio, can override what that registry returns, and a holder's exposure to the multiplier is equally total and passive — it is set by the issuer's registry, and the DTF simply reads it. Read on **2026-09-16**, the pause flag was `false` for all seven of MAG7's constituents. Section 9.6, *Market hours, 24/7 trading, and the 24/5 oracle*, gives the registry address, the reads behind it and the caveat on their provenance; Section 9.4 gives the dividend-reinvestment mechanism that makes one of the seven multipliers differ from 1.

### 13.14 Market-hours risk: a 24/7 token marked against a 24/5 oracle

MAG7 and its underlying tokens trade continuously; the mark behind them does not. Base's own product page advertises the first half — *"Trade tokenized stocks any hour of any day, including weekends and US market holidays"* — while the Chainlink feeds behind the seven c-tokens run **24/5**, updating during market hours on a 0.5% price deviation or at least every 24 hours and simply **holding the last value** off-hours, so `updatedAt` stops advancing while the contract stays callable. **This is not only an off-hours phenomenon** — Section 9.6 records a feed running 2 hours 45 minutes stale in the middle of a US regular session, which is exactly what a deviation rule produces in a quiet tape. **The token trades against a mark that is allowed to stand still**, and overnight, at weekends and on US market holidays the gap is wider by construction.

Two consequences travel with it. **Primary creation and redemption at the Coinbase layer are windowed, even though secondary trading is not** — the Issuer can buy and sell the underlying only on a defined Business Day, and the Reserve app imposes no market-hours window of its own, so **do not assume 24/7 mint-and-redeem parity with the underlying market.** And **corporate actions split the two layers**, because *"Mint and redeem pause offchain, but the token is not paused onchain, so transfers are not blocked."* Section 9.6 carries the prospectus and Base quotations behind both.

### 13.15 Composition and discretion risk: no criteria, no delisting rule, a 24-hour clock

MAG7's methodology is the least constrained in Reserve's line-up, and that cuts both ways. **There is no selection screen and nothing for a constituent to fail** — the roster is seven companies named in the `mandate()` string, with no market-cap minimum, no eligibility filter and no per-name cap, so where a rules-based index removes a constituent that stops meeting the rules, MAG7 has no rules for a constituent to stop meeting. There is no published rule for an acquisition or a delisting at either the DTF layer or the issuer's — two stacked discretionary gaps, and neither has a stated answer — and the two-month cadence is a sentence in `mandate()` that nothing onchain enforces, times or triggers.

**What MAG7 holds is a governance outcome on a 24-hour clock, not a property of the code**: the optimistic path whitelists exactly one function, `startRebalance`, which takes the entire token list and every weight as its arguments, and it executes after a **4-hour veto delay plus a 20-hour veto window** unless holders of at least **1% of vote-locked RSR** vote against it, with no timelock queue. Opening the auctions, meanwhile, has never been permissionless in practice. Section 7.5, *Who can change the basket, and how fast*, sets out the mechanism and Section 8.10 the configuration.

Before relying on the seven names and weights printed anywhere in this document, **read the live basket on app.reserve.org.**

### 13.16 Dividend, corporate-action and termination risk

A MAG7 holder **never receives a dividend, never votes a share, and is exposed to a chain of issuer discretion over corporate actions.** No cash ever reaches you at any layer — distributions are reinvested into the multiplier and the Deposit Ratio after a **5% distribution fee on the gross value** and up to **30% US withholding**, a material leakage that is invisible in the DTF's own fee table. None of the issuer's rates is fixed; it may change them on **thirty days' notice**. Corporate actions are determined unilaterally under an open-ended *"Adjustment Event"* and are binding on all Holders, subscription rights may be **allowed to lapse**, and the Issuer may terminate all Securities *"in its sole and absolute discretion"*, with a Holder's entitlement reallocated away after **90 days** — a total-loss path unrelated to the share price. Every quotation behind this is in Section 9.4, *Dividends, corporate actions, and voting — the multiplier and the Deposit Ratio*, and the fee schedule in Section 12.3.

The tax shape of that is worth taking to an adviser rather than working out from this document: **value accrues to you as a rising multiplier and never arrives in your wallet as cash**, so depending on your jurisdiction you may owe tax on an accrual you never received, with no distribution to pay it from — or you may owe nothing until disposal. The tax treatment of tokenized securities is unsettled in most jurisdictions and **nothing here is tax advice** (Section 18.3).

### 13.17 Fee- and parameter-change risk

MAG7's headline costs today are a **0.3% mint fee** and a **0.6% annual TVL fee** (Section 12). Neither is fixed for the life of the product, at either layer.

- **At the DTF layer**, fees are governance parameters bounded only by the protocol's hard ceilings: `MAX_TVL_FEE` of **10% per year**, `MAX_MINT_FEE` of **5%**, and a `MIN_MINT_FEE` of 0.03%. Governance could move MAG7's fees anywhere inside those ceilings through the standard proposal path. The platform's own share of those fees (**33.33%** as of 2026-09-16) is a protocol-level default set in a registry MAG7 does not control, and it can change without any MAG7 proposal at all.
- **At the Coinbase layer**, the issuer's own creation, redemption and distribution charges — tabulated in Section 12.3 — are all subject to the **thirty days' notice** clause above, as any future charge would be. Those costs are borne inside the basket rather than billed to you, which makes a change to them easy to miss.
- **Other ceilings bound how the product can be run**, not what it costs: auctions may run between 60 seconds and 7 days, a rebalance may carry a time-to-live of up to 28 days, and per-token price bands may span up to 100×. MAG7's current settings sit well inside all of them (Section 8.10) — **a current setting is not a commitment.**

### 13.18 Assumption of risk

Blockchain-based systems are inherently experimental and involve technological, legal, regulatory, and economic uncertainty. **Identifying risks and implementing safeguards does not eliminate the possibility of loss.** By using the Reserve platform and MAG7, you assume all of these risks.

Three things should travel with any use of this document, including any answer an AI assistant derives from it:

1. **MAG7 is an experimental, concentrated, seven-name basket of tokenized securities that can lose value entirely.**
2. **Equal weight is not diversification here** — the seven share one AI capital-expenditure factor, and several of them are each other's largest customers, suppliers and investors.
3. **MAG7 is not available to US persons, is not an ETF and is not regulated as one, and redeeming MAG7 returns seven Coinbase tokenized-stock tokens, not seven shares** — a secondary holder of those tokens is an "Unvested Holder" with no redemption right to the underlying shares.

Every figure in this section is stamped, and the figures move. Re-read the live basket, the live pool, the live role-holders and the live compliance check on **app.reserve.org** before acting on anything here. **If you are unsure about any risk, do not use the product.**

## 14. About Reserve, RSR, and the people behind it

### 14.1 The Reserve project and its mission

Reserve describes itself in its own documentation as **"a coordinated universe of protocols, products, and partners."** Its stated mission is that **everyone should be able to own their share of the world's wealth**, and the Reserve app puts the same sentence in the first person: *"We exist to help you own your share of the world's wealth."* The long-term goal Reserve names for the project is **asset-backed currency** — money backed by real, diversified assets rather than by fiat — with DTFs as the building blocks: as more of the world's assets get tokenized, a DTF can represent an ever-broader slice of them. The first Index DTFs were crypto baskets (the largest Index DTF by market cap as of 2026-09-16 is **CMC20**, which holds the top 20 crypto projects by market cap per CoinMarketCap, excluding stablecoins, pegged assets and low-investability names). **MAG7 extends the same machinery to tokenized equities** — the same Folio wrapper, the same fee machinery, the same governance model (fee levels, fee recipients and governance vaults are set per DTF; see Section 14.8) — with seven Coinbase Tokenized Stocks on Base in the basket instead of crypto assets (Section 9).

### 14.2 The platform: the Reserve app and the two protocols

The **Reserve app** at **app.reserve.org** — sometimes called **"Reserve Register,"** after its open-source frontend repository `reserve-protocol/register` — is a permissionless decentralized application for minting, redeeming, vote-locking and governing DTFs. Under it sit two separate open-source protocols: the **Index Protocol**, which powers Index DTFs such as MAG7, and the **Yield Protocol**, which powers Yield DTFs (Section 14.7). They are different codebases with different repositories, different risk structures and different roles for RSR; they are not two views of one system. Reserve's own documentation notes that the app does **not yet support permissionless creation of Index DTFs** — the live FAQ says the Index DTF deployment UI "is still under construction" — so while minting, redeeming and governing are permissionless today, launching a new Index DTF through the app is not.

On the project's age and backing, the only first-party statement this document relies on is the app's own **About Reserve** block, which says Reserve **has been operating since 2018** with backing from **Coinbase, Sam Altman and Peter Thiel**. *(The app copy is the source, and it is Reserve's self-description, not an independent verification.)* Note that Coinbase appears here as a named backer of Reserve **and** — through a separate Abu Dhabi entity — as the issuer of the tokenized stocks MAG7 holds. That overlap is a disclosure item, and Section 15 treats it as one.

On security, Reserve's published third-party reviews of the Index Protocol number five — four on its security page plus a fifth in the protocol repository's `audits/` directory — alongside a **$10,000,000** Cantina bug bounty; **the published coverage stops at Folio 4.0.0 and MAG7 runs Folio 5.0.0**, which shipped on 2025-12-10 with no published audit report of its own, and the Reserve Optimistic Governor repository carries no `audits/` directory either. Section 8.8 has the detail, including why a standing-partner statement is not a published report, and Section 13.6 has the consequence.

### 14.3 RSR (Reserve Rights), the ecosystem token

**RSR** is the Reserve ecosystem's governance and value-accrual token, with a **fixed maximum supply of 100 billion**. Reserve's documentation gives it three roles:

1. **Vote-locking on Index DTFs** — RSR is the default governance token, and locking it confers voting power over a DTF's basket, parameters and upgrades, plus a share of that DTF's fees where the DTF routes them to the vault. MAG7 has that routing, but the conversion step that would deliver it is permissionless and has not been run to date, so nothing has accrued to vote-lockers yet (Section 8.9). **This is the role that applies to MAG7** (Sections 8.9 and 14.5).
2. **Staking on Yield DTFs** — providing first-loss overcollateralization in exchange for a share of yield, and slashable if a collateral asset defaults. **This does not apply to MAG7** (Section 14.7).
3. **A deflationary sink** — the platform's share of every Index DTF's mint and TVL fees is used to market-buy RSR and burn it (Section 14.6).

**RSR is a separate token from MAG7.** Its canonical contract on **Base** is **`0xaB36452DbAC151bE02b16Ca17d8919826072f64a`** (18 decimals), which is the standard bridged token: its `l1Token()` is the Ethereum RSR contract `0x320623b8E4fF03373931769A31Fc52A4E78B5d70` and its `bridge()` is Base's standard bridge `0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000010`. Its Base supply was **5,672,661,833.45 RSR** as of 2026-09-16 (illustrative and approximate; it moves with bridging). On BNB Smart Chain, RSR is `0x23f72a3Db61D6CB8aBE5d9AF1Ac4B6c99327bFee` — **that is the address PHOTON and its siblings use, and it is the wrong one for anything on Base.** Owning MAG7 does not require owning RSR, and **this document is not a solicitation to buy RSR**; RSR is described here because MAG7's fee stream is routed partly to vote-lockers, through the unrun conversion described above (Section 8.9), and partly to the RSR burn.

### 14.4 Who operates what

- **ABC Labs, LLC** operates reserve.org and app.reserve.org. Its own terms say so plainly: *"Reserve.org and app.reserve.org (together, the 'Sites') are operated by ABC Labs, LLC ('ABC Labs')."* **ABC Labs is not a bank, broker-dealer, investment adviser, or other regulated financial intermediary, and is not registered with the SEC, the CFTC, or any other financial regulator.** Its careers page is at `https://www.abclabs.co/about#openroles`.
- **ABC Labs disclaims having launched any DTF.** From the app's own disclosures, verbatim: *"ABC Labs has neither created nor deployed any DTF and your ability to interact with specific DTFs via the Website in no way suggests that a DTF is endorsed by ABC Labs. In fact, ABC Labs assumes no liability for your use of the Website and interaction with the Reserve protocol, as covered in the Terms and Conditions."* The app is one of several ways to reach the protocol, not the protocol itself.
- **Confusion Capital** is, per Reserve's documentation, the entity named in January 2024 to manage funding for the Reserve Ecosystem, **including Best Friend Finance and ABC Labs**. It also administers the RSR Slower Wallet (Section 14.6).
- **Coinbase Onchain SPV Ltd** — an ADGM company, number **37941** — is the **third-party issuer of the tokenized stocks MAG7 holds**, with **Onchain Marketplace Limited** (ADGM 17243) as Tokenisation Entity, CSD and Reporting Entity, **Alpaca Securities LLC** as custodian and broker, and the **FSRA of ADGM** as regulator. **None of these entities is Reserve, and none of them has created, endorsed or is responsible for MAG7.** Their terms, their fees and their eligibility rules are their own, and they are the layer that decides who may ultimately hold the underlying (Sections 9 and 11).
- **MAG7's onchain roles sit with multisig Safes whose controllers are not publicly tagged.** No block explorer carries a public label for any of them and no public source attributes them to any named entity, so this document names none. What *is* onchain and checkable is the overlap: MAG7's Guardian contract, one of its two Auction Launcher / Brand Manager Safes, its Optimistic Proposer Safe and its deployer EOA are the same addresses recorded in Reserve's published reference documents for the BNB Smart Chain DTFs (Section 8.10). Section 15 sets out the resulting conflicts, including the 20% of gross fee revenue that flows to a 1-of-1 Safe controlled by the same Safe that holds MAG7's operational roles.

### 14.5 Governance token note

MAG7, like other Index DTFs, uses **RSR as its governance token, via vote-locking**. That means **RSR vote-lockers — not MAG7 holders as such — govern MAG7's parameters**, through the onchain process described in Section 8. Holding MAG7 gives you no vote.

The mechanism on Base is a vault called **"Vote-Locked Reserve Rights" (vlRSR)** at **`0x2F0D6538807a77d4AdDCd4b4DAf214Ea2E818E3D`**, whose underlying asset is RSR on Base. Three properties matter and are commonly got wrong:

- **Locked balance counts 1-for-1.** Reserve's live documentation is explicit: *"When tokens are locked, the entire balance counts 1-for-1 toward governance power."* **There is no "amount × lock time" multiplier** — longer locks do not buy more votes, and the deployed vault contract agrees: there is no lock-time term and no multiplier anywhere in it. Its only time parameter is the flat unstaking delay below, which is the same for every locker.
- **The lock is a 7-day unstaking delay, not a term commitment.** `unstakingDelay()` reads exactly **604,800 seconds**, and an unstake in progress can be cancelled by its owner, which redeposits the tokens and resumes voting.
- **Vote-locked RSR on an Index DTF is never slashable.** First-loss capital and slashing are Yield-Protocol machinery (Section 14.7).

As of 2026-09-16 18:17 UTC, read directly from the vault contract, the vault's vote supply was **33,691,556.89 vlRSR** and its vlRSR→RSR exchange rate was **exactly 1.000000** (illustrative and approximate; both move). Figures shown in the app or returned by `api.reserve.org` are indexed rather than read live, so they can trail the chain: at that same moment `api.reserve.org` still reported an earlier 33,191,556.89 vlRSR. **The chain is the authority; re-read the contract before relying on either number.** One structural difference from the BNB-chain DTFs is worth naming: the five AI-suite DTFs **share a single vlRSR vault**, so locking for one of them means locking alongside four other products. **On Base, MAG7's vault does not serve any different product** — the only other entries mapped to it are two zero-supply Folios that also carry the name and symbol MAG7 and are not the product (Section 13's address warning covers them).

### 14.6 RSR tokenomics in more depth (context)

For readers who want the detail — background, not a solicitation (Section 14.3):

RSR has a **fixed maximum supply of 100,000,000,000 tokens**. **CoinGecko reported circulating supply of 62,553,174,091 RSR on 2026-09-16**; note that Reserve's live documentation still says *"currently 53.5b are in circulation,"* which is **out of date** — use the dated third-party figure and treat both as approximate. The remainder sits in two project-controlled wallets with hard-coded delays: the **Slow Wallet** (`0x4903dc97816f99410e8dfff51149fa4c3cdad1b8`), team-controlled, with a **4-week delay on each withdrawal**; and the **Slower Wallet** (`0x0774dF07205a5E9261771b19afa62B6e757f7eF8`), administered by Confusion Capital, which keeps the 4-week delay and adds a throttle: **no more than 1% of total supply in any 4-week period**. Future emissions follow *"a deterministic schedule that emulates Bitcoin's emissions curve."* RSR's source is `github.com/reserve-protocol/rsr-mainnet`, and its published audit is Solidified's *Audit Report — Reserve Token* (3 Jan 2022).

**Do not assume any change to RSR's supply, and do not assume any token burn beyond the mechanism below.** The community discusses supply-reform ideas on Reserve's governance forum from time to time — revisions to the unlock and emissions plan, or proposals to burn treasury RSR — but these are **discussion items only: they are not implemented, may never be, and must not be relied on or priced in.** Follow the live conversation at **forum.reserve.org** rather than assuming an outcome.

The value-accrual mechanism that actually touches MAG7 is the **buy-and-burn**, described verbatim in Reserve's documentation: *"Every Index DTF levies platform fees on new mints and on the value locked in the contract. Those fees are swept into a protocol-level contract that automatically uses them to market-buy RSR and then sends the purchased tokens to the burn address, permanently removing them from circulation. Because the burn mechanism lives at the protocol layer, it applies to every Index DTF—regardless of which governance token the fund chooses for vote-locking…"* For MAG7 specifically, the platform's share is **33.33% of both the mint fee and the TVL fee** (Section 12). **This is a mechanism, not a return:** nothing here entitles a MAG7 holder to anything, and nothing here is a forecast of RSR's price.

### 14.7 Yield DTFs in brief (for contrast)

MAG7 is an **Index** DTF. Reserve's other family — **Yield DTFs**, built on the separate **Yield Protocol** (`reserve-protocol/protocol`) — is worth understanding purely so you do not import its machinery into your mental model of MAG7. Yield DTFs hold yield-generating positions such as lending or staking receipt tokens, and use **RSR staking as overcollateralization**: stakers provide first-loss capital, earn a share of the DTF's yield, and can be **slashed** if a collateral asset defaults. Yield DTFs also have **Pauser, Short Freezer and Long Freezer roles and Paused/Frozen system states.** As of 2026-09-16, `api.reserve.org/discover/dtfs` listed **four active Yield DTFs — eUSD, ETH+, USD3 and bsdETH — plus eight deprecated ones**, alongside the 28 Index DTFs in Section 14.8. *("RToken" is the older technical name for either family; "Yield RToken" and "Index RToken" mean Yield DTF and Index DTF.)*

**None of that applies to MAG7.** If you read about RSR "staking," "slashing," "first-loss capital," "pause," "freeze," or "self-healing" during a depeg, that is Yield-DTF machinery. MAG7 holds tokenized equities, **pays no yield**, uses **no RSR overcollateralization**, and — as the deployed v5 Folio ABI confirms — **has no pause and no freeze function at all**. Its main emergency lever is `deprecateFolio()`, which puts the Folio into **redemption-only** mode: no new mints and no rebalancing, but redemption stays open; the admin can also claw back an in-flight trusted fill (`emergencyCloseTrustedFill`) and switch off permissionless bidding (`setBidsEnabled`), which restricts trading to trusted fillers (Section 8). MAG7 uses RSR **only** for vote-locking governance.

### 14.8 The wider Reserve DTF line-up

**MAG7 is not part of the "Reserve Thematic AI DTF Suite."** That suite is five market-cap-weighted, quarterly-rebalanced equity DTFs on **BNB Smart Chain**, holding **Ondo Global Markets** tokenized stocks; MAG7 is an equal-weight, seven-name DTF on **Base** holding **Coinbase** tokenized stocks, with a stated two-month cadence. It is a **separate product**, and any document or index that folds it into the suite is wrong.

As of **2026-09-16**, `api.reserve.org/discover/dtfs` listed **40 DTFs of all kinds: 28 Index DTFs — 18 active and 10 deprecated — and 12 Yield DTFs.** MAG7 is one of the 18 active Index DTFs. **Every market cap below is illustrative and approximate as of 2026-09-16 ~16:47 UTC**; caps move every block and the active/deprecated flags change by governance. - **The same class of risk applies to every DTF in the set** (Section 13). For the authoritative, live details on any of them, go to **app.reserve.org**.
